AGZT-Lectures on formal multiple zeta values
A. Burmester, N. Confurius, U. Kühn

Abstract
Formal multiple zeta values allow to study multiple zeta values by algebraic methods in a way that the open question about their transcendence is circumvented. In this note we show that Hoffman’s basis conjecture for formal multiple zeta values is implied by the free odd generation conjecture for the double shuffle Lie algebra. We use the concept of a post-Lie structure for a convenient approach to the multiplication on the double shuffle group. From this, we get a coaction on the algebra of formal multiple zeta values. This in turn allows us to follow the proof of Brown’s celebrated and unconditional theorem for the same result in the context of motivic multiple zeta values. We need the free odd generation conjecture twice: at first it gives a formula for the graded dimensions and secondly it is a key to derive a lift of the Zagier formula to the formal context.



1
Introduction

Multiple zeta values (MZVs) are real numbers defined as the convergent series

\zeta(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d})=\sum\limits_{n_{1}>\cdots>n_{d}>0}\dfrac{1}{n_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots n_{d}^{k_{d}}},

(1)

where
k_{i}
are positive integers and the first component
k_{1}
is strictly greater than
1
. These values were first considered by Euler in the 18th century and since then they have been studied in
various contexts in number theory, knot theory and the theory of mixed Tate motives. There is Hoffman’s list of all related publications
[
28
]
. MZVs form a
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\Z
, which is contained in
\mathbb{R}
.
One of the most challenging open question in the study of MZVs is the identification of all relations among them, even the question whether
\Z
is graded by the weight is still open.

One of the important properties of MZVs is their representation in terms of iterated integral as follows:

\zeta(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d})=\int\limits_{1>t_{1}>\cdots>t_{n}>0}\omega_{1}(t_{1})\cdots\omega_{n}(t_{n}),

(2)

where
n=k_{1}+\cdots+k_{d}
is the weight of the MZV, and
\omega_{i}(t_{i})=dt_{i}/(1-t_{i})
if
i\in\{k_{1},k_{1}+k_{2},\dotsc,k_{1}+\cdots+k_{d}\}
, and
\omega_{i}(t_{i})=dt_{i}/t_{i}
otherwise. The series representation (
1
) and the integral representation (
2
) provide two different ways of expanding the product of two MZVs as linear combinations of MZVs, resulting in two distinct combinatorial interpretations. The equality of the products then allows us to generate a large family of relations among MZVs called double shuffle relations. Nevertheless, these relations are not sufficient to capture all linear relations, for instance, the well-known identity
\zeta(2,1)=\zeta(3)
due to Euler cannot be derived from them. In order to remedy this, Ihara, Kaneko, and Zagier extended the double shuffle relations by appropriate regularisations
\zeta_{\ast}(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d})
and
\zeta_{\shuffle}(k_{1},\dotsc,k_{d})
for the divergent series and integrals respectivly. A comparision theorem for these two regularisations allowed them to introduce the so-called extended double shuffle relations (EDS), which are widely believed to determine all linear relations among MZVs (see Conjecture 1 in
[
31
]
).

There are two ways to study MZV’s algebraically either by means of the formal multiple zeta values or by means of the motivic multiple zeta values.

The algebra formal multiple zeta values
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
is the algebra spanned by symbols
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k_{1},...,k_{d})
, which satisfy exactly the EDS and no other relations. The work of
Racinet
[
38
]
allows to study
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
in the context of Hopf algebras. By construction there is a surjective algebra morphism
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}\to\Z
given by

\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k_{1},...,k_{d})\to\zeta_{\shuffle}(k_{1},...,k_{d}).

The algebra of motivic multiple zeta values
\Z^{\mathfrak{m}}
introduced and studied intensively by Goncharov, Deligne and Brown
(see e.g.
[
17
]
,
[
26
]
,
[
16
]
,
[
9
]
)
is a Hopf algebra of functions on a certain group scheme associated to the fundamental group of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
. It is spanned by symbols
I^{\mathfrak{m}}(\varepsilon_{0};\varepsilon_{1},...,\varepsilon_{n};\varepsilon_{n+1})
, where
\varepsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, modulo some relations in such a way that the period map
\Z^{\mathfrak{m}}\to\Z
given by

I^{\mathfrak{m}}(\varepsilon_{0};\varepsilon_{1},...,\varepsilon_{n};\varepsilon_{n+1})\to\int\limits_{\varepsilon_{n+1}>t_{1}>\cdots>t_{n}>\varepsilon_{0}}\omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}(t_{1})\cdots\omega_{\varepsilon_{n}}(t_{n})

is a surjective algebra morphism. For more details we refer to the book of Burgos-Gil and Fresan
[
10
]
.

Both approaches fit in the following abstract setting:

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

(3)

Here
G=\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{g}))
is a graded, pro-unipotent group scheme,
\mathfrak{g}
its Lie algebra,
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{g}
and the Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}
has two descriptions.
It equals the graded dual of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
as well as the Hopf algebra of functions on
G
. Finally
\operatorname{Indec}(\mathcal{A})
is the Lie coalgebra of the indecomposable elements of
\mathcal{A}
.
In the formal setup, we have
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}/\penalty(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2))
and Racinet denotes
G
by
\operatorname{DM}_{0}
and the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
by
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
.
In the motivic setup
\mathcal{A}=\Z^{\mathfrak{m}}/\penalty(\zeta^{\mathfrak{m}}(2))
and
G
relates to the Galois group of the category of mixed Tate motives.

Explicit calculations show

\Delta_{\operatorname{DM}_{0}}=\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}},

thus for both approaches we have the same formulae for the coproduct.
We like to emphasize the fact that our approach
to the coproduct
\Delta_{\operatorname{DM}_{0}}
relies on the general theory of post-Lie algebras together with the work of Racinet, whereas
the original definition of the Goncharov coproduct
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}
in
[
26
]

was based on topological considerations for the path algebra. The latter is directly related to the representation of multiple zeta values by iterated integrals, which is a key to motivic multiple zeta values.
A small modification of this coproduct enables us to obtain the first important step in these lecture notes

Theorem 1.1
.

Set
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}/\penalty\big(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)\big)
. There is a well-defined coaction

\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}:\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}\otimes\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}},

which is given by the same formulae as the Brown-Goncharov coaction for
the motivic multiple zeta values.

Central for this notes is the following well-known conjecture for
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
, which is motivated by conjectures of Deligne (
[
17
]
) and Y. Ihara (
[
32
, p. 300]
)
in the context of certain Galois actions and of Drinfeld
[
19
]
on his Grothendieck-Teichmüller Lie algebra. By work of Furusho
[
24
]
, we know that the Grothendieck-Teichmüller Lie algebra embedds into
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
.

Conjecture 1.2
.

The double shuffle Lie algebra
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
is a free Lie algebra with exactly one generator in each odd weight
w\geq 3
, i. e.

\mathfrak{dm}_{0}\simeq\Lie(S),

where the set
S
is given by
S=\{s_{3},s_{5},\ldots\}
.
We call this conjecture the free odd generation conjecture.

The main results we present in this lecture notes are the following.

Proposition 1.3
.

Assume the free odd generation conjecture  holds for
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
, then

1.

Zagier’s conjecture holds for
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
, i.e.

\sum_{w\geq 0}\dim(\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}_{w})\,x^{w}=\frac{1}{1-x^{2}-x^{3}},

where
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}_{w}
is the subspace spanned by formal MZVs of weight
w
.

2.

The formal zeta values
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)
and
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2r+1)
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, are non-zero modulo products and algebraically independent.

3.

The Kernel conjecture
5.52
holds for
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
, i.e.

\ker D_{<N}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}_{N}=\mathbb{Q}\,\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(N).

Using this proposition
1
1

1

In the motivic setup, the first claim is a theorem of Terasoma and Deligne-Goncharov. The second and third claim
are consequences of the construction of motivic multiple zeta values
[
9
]
,
[
10
]
. In the long end they rely on Borel’s theorem on the algebraic
K
-theory of
\mathbb{Q}
.
it is not difficult to follow the lines of Brown’s proof to derive the following results.

Theorem 1.4
.

If the free odd generation conjecture  holds, then all
the
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})
with
k_{i}\in\{2,3\}
are linearly independent.

The dimension of the space spanned by the formal multiple zeta values from the above theorem in a fixed weight are the same as the ones we expect for all in Zagier’s conjecture, therefore we get as corollary the verification of the Hoffman basis conjecture for the formal multiple zeta values.

Corollary 1.5
.

If the free odd generation conjecture  holds, then
the
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})
with
k_{i}\in\{2,3\}
form a basis for
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
as a vector space.

These notes are based on a series of talks we gave at the

A
rithmetische
G
eometrie und
Z
ahlen
T
heorie Seminar

at the Universität Hamburg in the summer term of 2023. We like to thanks the audience for helpful remarks, which improved our understanding and this presentation.
Our motivation to study Brown’s theorem in the context of formal multiple zeta values is that for multiple
q
-zeta values and for multiple Eisenstein series similar results either hold or conjecturally hold
[
4
]
. Recent progress in that directions can be found in
[
13
]
,
[
12
]
,

[
5
]
,
[
3
]
.

Special thanks also go to Henrik Bachmann, Jose Burgos-Gil, Pierre Lochak, Dominique Manchon, Leila Schneps for various fruitful discussions related to these projects.

2
Algebraic background

We provide the general algebraic constructions, which we will use in all following sections.

2.1
Hopf algebras

We start by a short presentation of Hopf algebras and their behaviour under duality. Detailed introductions into the theory of Hopf algebras can be found in
[
14
]
,
[
23
]
, and
[
35
]
. In the following, let
R
be any commutative ring.

Definition 2.1
.

A
Hopf algebra
over
R
is a tuple
(H,m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
, where
H
is an
R
-algebra with the multiplication
m:H\otimes H\to H
and the unit map
\eta:R\to H
,

\displaystyle\Delta:H\to H\otimes H\qquad(coproduct),\hskip 71.13188pt\varepsilon:H\to R\qquad(counit)

are
R
-algebra morphisms, and

S:H\to H\qquad\qquad(antipode)

is a
R
-module morphism, such that the following compatibility conditions hold

(i)

coassociativity:

(\operatorname{id}\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta=(\Delta\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta,

(ii)

counitarity:

m\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes\varepsilon)\circ\Delta=m\circ(\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta=\operatorname{id},

(iii)

antipode property:

m\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)\circ\Delta=m\circ(S\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta=\eta\circ\varepsilon.

In the following, we will often omit the unit map, the counit or the antipode, if they are clear from the context or the explicit shape does not matter.

Definition 2.2
.

We call a Hopf algebra
(H,m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)

graded
if there is a decomposition

H=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}H_{i},

where each
H_{i}
is a free
R
-submodule of finite rank, such that

(i)

m(H_{i}\otimes H_{j})\subset H_{i+j}
for
i,j\geq 0
,

(ii)

\Delta(H_{n})\subset\bigoplus_{i+j=n}H_{i}\otimes H_{j}
for
n\geq 0
,

(iii)

S(H_{i})\subset H_{i}
for
i\geq 0
.

In this case, we have

\displaystyle\eta(R)\subset H_{0},\qquad\varepsilon(H_{i})=0\quad\text{for }i\geq 1.

Similarly, we call modules, algebras, and coalgebras graded if they satisfy the corresponding subsets of the above conditions.

Definition 2.3
.

(i) Let
A
be an
R
-module equipped with a
descending filtration
, i.e., there is a chain of submodules

A=\operatorname{Fil}^{(0)}A\supset\operatorname{Fil}^{(1)}A\supset\operatorname{Fil}^{(2)}A\supset\operatorname{Fil}^{(3)}A\supset\dots\ .

The
completion

\widehat{A}
of
A
with respect to this filtration is defined by the inverse limit

\widehat{A}=\varprojlim_{j}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.68945pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.43945pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.3839pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.76909pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}}.

If
\widehat{A}=A
, then
A
is called a
complete

R
-module.

The completion
\widehat{A}
of
A
is also a filtered
R
-module via

\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}\widehat{A}=\varprojlim_{k>j}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.68945pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.68945pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(k)}A}$}}{\raisebox{4.43945pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.43945pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(k)}A}$}}{\raisebox{3.3839pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.3839pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(k)}A}$}}{\raisebox{2.76909pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.76909pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(k)}A}$}}}.

Proposition 2.4
.

Assume that
A=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}A_{i}
is a graded
R
-module. Then
A
admits a descending filtration given by
\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A=\bigoplus_{i\geq j}A_{i}
. Since
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.68945pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.43945pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.3839pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.76909pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}}=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{j-1}A_{i}
, the completion of
A
is

\widehat{A}=\varprojlim_{j}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.68945pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.43945pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.3839pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.76909pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}}=\prod_{i\geq 0}A_{i}.

The completion
\widehat{A}
is filtered by
\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}\widehat{A}=\prod_{i\geq j}A_{i}
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
(H,m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
be a graded Hopf algebra. By extending the maps
m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S
of
H
to the completed module
\widehat{H}
, one obtains
completed Hopf algebra
of
H
.

The completed Hopf algebra
(\widehat{H},m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
is filtered, i.e., one has for all
i\geq 0

\displaystyle m(\operatorname{Fil}^{(i)}H\otimes\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}H)\subset\operatorname{Fil}^{(i+j)}H,\qquad\Delta(\operatorname{Fil}^{(i)}H)\subset\sum_{m+n=i}\operatorname{Fil}^{(m)}H\otimes\operatorname{Fil}^{(n)}H,\qquad

\displaystyle S(\operatorname{Fil}^{(i)}H)\subset\operatorname{Fil}^{(i)}H.

Evidently, we have the same construction for modules, algebras, and coalgebras.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
A
be a filtered
R
-module. Then the
associated graded module

\operatorname{gr}A
is defined by

\operatorname{gr}A=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.68945pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.68945pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j+1)}A}$}}{\raisebox{4.43945pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.43945pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j+1)}A}$}}{\raisebox{3.3839pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.3839pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j+1)}A}$}}{\raisebox{2.76909pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.76909pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j+1)}A}$}}}.

One has
\operatorname{gr}A=\operatorname{gr}\widehat{A}
. In particular, if
A
is a graded module, then
\operatorname{gr}\widehat{A}=A
.

If
A
is a filtered
R
-module and all quotients
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.68945pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.43945pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.3839pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{A}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.76909pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}A}$}}}
are free modules of finite rank, then the module
\operatorname{gr}M
is graded in the sense of Definition
2.2
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
(H,m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
be a filtered Hopf algebra over
R
. Then the
associated graded Hopf algebra
is the
R
-module
\operatorname{gr}H
equipped with the induced maps by
m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon
and
S
.

As before, we define the associated graded for modules, algebras, and coalgebras in the same way.

Hopf algebras behave nicely under duality pairings as introduced in
[
1
, Chapter 2, Section 2.1]
.

Definition 2.8
.

Two
R
-modules
A
and
B
are
dual
, if there is an
R
-linear map

(\cdot\mid\cdot):A\otimes B\to R,

such that

(i)

if
(a\mid b)=0
for all
a\in A
, then
b=0
,

(ii)

if
(a\mid b)=0
for all
b\in B
, then
a=0
.

In this case,
(\cdot\mid\cdot)
is called the
duality pairing
of
A
and
B
.

Let
A
and
B
be graded
R
-modules. If there is a duality pairing
(\cdot\mid\cdot):A\otimes B\to R
, such that

(A_{i}\mid B_{j})=0\quad\text{for all }i\neq j,

then
A
and
B
are
graded dual
. In this case, we say that
(\cdot\mid\cdot)
is a
graded duality pairing
.

Example 2.9
.

(i) Let
A
be a free
R
-module of finite rank. Usually, the dual module is defined by

A^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}_{R\operatorname{-lin}}(A,R).

The modules
A
and
A^{*}
are also dual in the sense of Definition
2.8
, the duality pairing is given by

\displaystyle(\cdot\mid\cdot):A^{*}\otimes A

\displaystyle\to R,

\displaystyle f\otimes a

\displaystyle\mapsto f(a).

(ii) Let
A
be a graded
R
-module. Then usually, its graded dual is defined by

A^{\vee}=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}A_{i}^{*}.

The modules
A
and
A^{\vee}
are also graded dual in the sense of Definition
2.8
, the graded duality pairing is given by

\displaystyle A^{\vee}\otimes A

\displaystyle\to R,

\displaystyle f_{i}\otimes a_{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}f_{i}(a_{j}),&\quad i=j,\\
0&\quad\text{else}\end{cases}\qquad(\text{where }f_{i}\in A_{i}^{*},\ a_{j}\in A_{j}).

In all following sections, we will use the notion
(-)^{\vee}
exclusively for the graded dual.

Let
A_{1},B_{1}
be dual
R
-modules for the pairing
(\cdot\mid\cdot)_{1}
,
A_{2},B_{2}
be dual
R
-modules for the pairing
(\cdot\mid\cdot)_{2}
, and
f:A_{1}\to A_{2}
be an
R
-linear map. The
dual map
to
f
is the unique
R
-linear map
g:B_{2}\to B_{1}
satisfying

(f(a),b)_{2}=(a,g(b))_{1}\qquad\text{ for all }a\in A_{1},\ b\in B_{2}.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
(H,m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
be a (graded) Hopf algebra over
R
. If
H^{\prime}
is an
R
-module (graded) dual to
H
, then
H^{\prime}
equipped with the dual maps of
m,\ \eta,\ \Delta,\ \varepsilon
and
S
is also a (graded) Hopf algebra over
R
. ∎

2.2
Hoffman’s quasi-shuffle Hopf algebras

We present a particular class of Hopf algebras, called quasi-shuffle Hopf algebras. Those we first introduced in
[
30
]
,
[
27
]
, and all results are taken from there. Let
R
be a commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra with unit.

Notation 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an alphabet, this means
\mathcal{A}
is a countable set whose elements are called
letters
. By
R\mathcal{A}
denote the
R
-module spanned by the letters of
\mathcal{A}
and let
R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle
be the free non-commutative algebra generated by the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
. The monic monomials in
R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle
are called
words
with letters in
\mathcal{A}
, the set of all words is
\mathcal{A}^{*}
. Moreover, we denote by
\mathbf{1}
the empty word.

The
length
of a word
w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
equals the number of its letters, i.e., the word
w=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}
with
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
has length
n
.
We introduce the
j
-th letter function

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}:\mathcal{A}^{*}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{A}\cup\{\mathbf{1}\},

\displaystyle a_{1}\cdots a_{n}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}a_{j}\quad&\text{ if }j\leq n,\\
\mathbf{1}&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

Instead of
w=\varepsilon_{1}(w)\varepsilon_{2}(w)\cdots\varepsilon_{n}(w)\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
, we will often just write
w=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}
where
n
is the length of
w
. Furthermore, we call
u=\varepsilon_{i_{1}}(w)\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}(w)
a
subword
of
w
if
i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq n
for some integer
k\geq 0
. A subword
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}
of
w
is called
strict
if
i_{j}+1=i_{j+1}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}
, i.e., if it consists of consecutive letters of
w
.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\diamond:R\mathcal{A}\times R\mathcal{A}\to R\mathcal{A}
be a commutative and associative product. Define the
quasi-shuffle product

\ast_{\diamond}
on
R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle
recursively by
\mathbf{1}\ast_{\diamond}w=w\ast_{\diamond}\mathbf{1}=w
and

\displaystyle au\ast_{\diamond}bv=a(u\ast_{\diamond}bv)+b(au\ast_{\diamond}v)+(a\diamond b)(u\ast_{\diamond}v)

for all
u,v,w\in R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle
and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
.

Note that the quasi-shuffle product
\ast_{\diamond}
can be equally defined recursively from the left and from the right, since both product expressions agree
[
44
, Theorem 9]
.

Example 2.13
.

Define

a\diamond b=0\qquad\text{for all }a,b\in\mathcal{A},

then we get the well-known
shuffle product
, which is usually denoted by
\shuffle
.

The
deconcatenation coproduct

\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}:R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\to R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\otimes R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle
is given for a word
w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}(w)=\sum_{w=uv}u\otimes v,

(4)

and the corresponding counit
\varepsilon:R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\to R
is given for a word
w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
by

\displaystyle\varepsilon(w)=\begin{cases}1,\quad&w=\mathbf{1},\\
0&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Theorem 2.14
.

(
[
30
, Theorem 3.1,3.2]
) The tuple
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}},\varepsilon)
is an associative, commutative Hopf algebra. ∎

An explicit formula for the antipode of the Hopf algebra
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}},\varepsilon)
is also given in
[
30
, Theorem 3.2]
.

For the shuffle algebra
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\shuffle)
, c.f. Example
2.13
, there is an explicit generating set. Choose a total ordering on the alphabet
\mathcal{A}
, then the lexicographic ordering defines a total ordering on the set of all words
\mathcal{A}^{*}
.

Definition 2.15
.

A word
w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}\backslash\{\mathbf{1}\}
is called a
Lyndon word
if we have for any non-trivial decomposition
w=uv
that
w<v
.

Theorem 2.16
.

(
[
40
, Theorem 4.9 (ii)]
) The shuffle algebra
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\shuffle)
is a free polynomial algebra generated by the Lyndon words of
\mathcal{A}
. ∎

We will see that all quasi-shuffle algebras over the same alphabet
\mathcal{A}
are isomorphic. In particular, the previous theorem holds for all quasi-shuffle algebras.

Let
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond})
be a quasi-shuffle algebra. By a
composition
of a positive integer
n
we mean an ordered sequence
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
, such that
i_{1}+\dots+i_{r}=n
. Let
w=a_{1}\dots a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{*}
be a word and
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})
a composition of
n
, then define

\displaystyle I[w]

\displaystyle=(a_{1}\diamond\dots\diamond a_{i_{1}})(a_{i_{1}+1}\diamond\dots\diamond a_{i_{1}+i_{2}})\dots(a_{i_{1}+\dots+i_{r-1}+1}\diamond\dots\diamond a_{n})

and

\displaystyle\exp_{\ast_{\diamond}}(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\text{ composition of }n}\frac{1}{i_{1}!\cdots i_{r}!}I[w],

\displaystyle\log_{\ast_{\diamond}}(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{r})\text{ composition of }n}\frac{(-1)^{n-r}}{i_{1}\cdots i_{r}}I[w].

Theorem 2.17
.

(
[
30
, Theorem 3.3]
) The map
\exp_{\ast_{\diamond}}
is a Hopf algebra isomorphism

\exp_{\ast_{\diamond}}:(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})\xrightarrow{\sim}(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond},\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}).

The inverse map is given by
\log_{\ast_{\diamond}}
. ∎

From Theorem
2.16
and
2.17
, one deduces the following.

Corollary 2.18
.

Any quasi-shuffle algebra
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond})
is a free polynomial algebra generated by the Lyndon words of
\mathcal{A}
.

We want to determine a dual of the quasi-shuffle Hopf algebra. Define a degree map on the letters in
\mathcal{A}
, such that
\deg(a)\geq 1
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
. This induces a grading on
R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle
by

\deg(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})=\deg(a_{1})+\dots+\deg(a_{n}),\qquad a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathcal{A}.

Denote by
R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle
the completion with respect to this grading. There is a duality pairing

\displaystyle(\cdot\mid\cdot):R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle\otimes R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle

\displaystyle\to R,

(5)

\displaystyle\phi\otimes w

\displaystyle\mapsto(\phi\mid w),

where
(\phi\mid w)
denotes the coefficient of
\phi\in R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle
in
w\in R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle
. We assume that
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond},\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})
is a graded Hopf algebra with respect to the above degree map (Definition
2.2
). Then the dual coproduct
\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}}:R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle\to R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle\otimes R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle
to
\ast_{\diamond}
with respect to the above duality pairing is given by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}}(\phi)=\sum_{u,v\in\mathcal{A}^{*}}(\phi\mid u\ast_{\diamond}v)\ u\otimes v.

(6)

Since the quasi-shuffle product
\ast_{\diamond}
is graded and the homogeneous subspaces of
R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle
are finite dimensional, each coefficient in the coproduct is finite.
Moreover, denote the concatenation product by
\operatorname{conc}
.

Theorem 2.19
.

The tuple
(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}},\varepsilon)
is a complete cocommutative Hopf algebra. It is dual to the quasi-shuffle Hopf algebra
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}},\varepsilon)
with respect to the pairing
(\cdot\mid\cdot)
given in (
5
). ∎

An explicit formula for the antipode of the Hopf algebra
(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}},\varepsilon)
is given in
[
30
, p. 9]
.

Example 2.20
.

For the shuffle product
\shuffle
given in Example
2.13
, the dual coproduct is given by

\Delta_{\shuffle}(a)=a\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes a\qquad\text{for all }a\in\mathcal{A}.

So
(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\shuffle})
is a cocommutative Hopf algebra.

Remark 2.21
.

The associated graded Hopf algebra (cf Definition
2.7
) to the completed Hopf algebra
(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}},\varepsilon)
is just
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}},\varepsilon)
. By construction,
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}},\varepsilon)
is exactly the graded dual to
(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond},\mathbf{1},\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}},\varepsilon)
in the sense of Example
2.9
.

2.3
The interaction of Hopf and Lie algebras

We review some basic results on the interplay of Hopf algebras, group schemes, and Lie algebras, which will be applied in the following sections.

We start with a basic example for a Hopf algebra, which occurs many times in the following. Let
R
be any fixed commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra with unit.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-])
be a Lie algebra over
R
. Then the
universal enveloping algebra
of
\mathfrak{g}
is

\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x-[x,y]\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x-[x,y]\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x-[x,y]\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x-[x,y]\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}\rangle}$}}},

where
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}\mathfrak{g}^{\otimes j}
is the
tensor algebra
. The product on
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
is induced by the concatenation product on the tensor algebra
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})
.

The universal enveloping algebra of a Lie algebra is naturally equipped with a Hopf algebra structure. Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-])
be any Lie algebra. Then the tensor algebra
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})
is a Hopf algebra with the coproduct
\Delta:\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})\otimes\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})
given by

\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x,\qquad x\in\mathfrak{g},

the counit
\varepsilon:\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})\to R
given by

\varepsilon(1)=1,\qquad\varepsilon(x)=0\quad\text{ for }x\in\mathfrak{g},

and the antipode
S:\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})
given by

S(x_{1}\cdots x_{n})=(-1)^{n}x_{n}\cdots x_{1},\qquad x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}.

Since
\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x-[x,y]\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}\rangle
is a Hopf ideal in
\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g})
, also
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
becomes a Hopf algebra with the induced coproduct, counit, and antipode.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-])
be a graded Lie algebra with
\operatorname{rank}L_{0}=0
. Then, the universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
admits a grading with respect to the Hopf algebra structure. The graded dual of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
is the symmetric algebra of the graded dual
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}

\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})={\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.00891pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.00891pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.75891pt}{$\textstyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75891pt}{$\textstyle{\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.8978pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.11111pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{T}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle x\otimes y-y\otimes x\mid x,y\in\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\rangle}$}}},

this means we have an algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})^{\vee}\simeq\mathcal{S}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}).

(7)

It is well-known that symmetric algebras are free polynomial algebras.

Example 2.23
.

Consider the free Lie algebra
\Lie_{R}(\mathcal{A})
over some alphabet
\mathcal{A}
. Then, the universal enveloping algebra is given by

\mathcal{U}(\Lie_{R}(\mathcal{A}))=(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\shuffle}),

and thus the graded dual is (cf. Example
2.20
)

\mathcal{U}(\Lie(\mathcal{A}))^{\vee}=(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}).

So by (
7
), there is an algebra isomorphism

(R\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\shuffle)\simeq(\mathcal{S}(\Lie_{R}(\mathcal{A})),\cdot).

Moreover,
\mathcal{U}(\Lie(\mathcal{A}))
is dual to
(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})
under the duality pairing given in (
5
) (cf Remark
2.21
).

We review grouplike, primitive and indecomposable elements of Hopf algebras and their relationship. For this, we fix a commutative ring
R
and a Hopf algebra
(H,m,\eta,\Delta,\varepsilon,S)
over
R
.

Definition 2.24
.

An element
x\in H\backslash\{0\}
is called
grouplike
if

\Delta(x)=x\otimes x.

The set of grouplike elements in
H
is denoted by
\operatorname{Grp}(H)
. An element
x\in H
is called
primitive
if it satisfies

\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x.

By
\Prim(H)
we denote the set of all primitive elements in
H
.

Theorem 2.25
.

The following holds.

(i)

The set
\operatorname{Grp}(H)
equipped with the product and the unit of
H
forms a group. For an element
x\in\operatorname{Grp}(H)
, the inverse element is given by
S(x)
.
Moreover, each grouplike element
x\in H
satisfies
\varepsilon(x)=1
.

(ii)

The set
\operatorname{Prim}(H)
equipped with the commutator bracket
[x,y]=m(x\otimes y)-m(y\otimes x)
is a Lie algebra. Furthermore, one has for each primitive element
x\in H
that
\varepsilon(x)=0
and
S(x)=-x
.

∎

Theorem 2.26
.

(Cartier-Quillen-Milnor-Moore) Let
H
be a graded cocommutative Hopf algebra, such that
\operatorname{rank}H_{0}=1
. Then there is a Hopf algebra isomorphism

H\simeq\mathcal{U}(\Prim(H)).

∎

By passing to completions, we are able to relate the grouplike and primitive elements via an exponential map.
Let
H=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}H_{j}
be a graded Hopf algebra and
\widehat{H}=\prod_{j\geq 0}H_{j}
its completion (Definition
2.21
). For any element
x\in\prod_{j\geq 1}H_{j}\subset\widehat{H}
, define

\exp_{H}(x)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\frac{1}{i!}x^{i},

where
x^{i}
means applying the product map of
H
exactly
(i-1)
-times to
x^{\otimes i}
.

Proposition 2.27
.

Let
H
be a graded Hopf algebra. Then there is a bijection

\displaystyle\Prim(\widehat{H})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{H}),

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp_{H}(x).

∎

Definition 2.28
.

The space of
indecomposables
of
H
is defined as

\Indec(H)={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\ker(\varepsilon)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\ker(\varepsilon)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\ker(\varepsilon)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\ker(\varepsilon)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\ker(\varepsilon)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.12001pt}{$\scriptstyle{\ker(\varepsilon)^{2}}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\ker(\varepsilon)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.33333pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\ker(\varepsilon)^{2}}$}}}.

Recall that if
H
is a graded Hopf algebra with
H=\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}H_{j}
, then we have

\ker(\varepsilon)=\bigoplus_{j\geq 1}H_{j}.

Define the corresponding Lie cobracket
\delta
to the coproduct
\Delta
of
H
as

\displaystyle\delta=(\operatorname{id}-t)\circ\Delta:H\to H\otimes H,

(8)

where
t:H\otimes H\to H\otimes H
simply permutes the tensor product factors.

Proposition 2.29
.

The Lie cobracket
\delta
defined in (
8
) induces a Lie coalgebra structure on the space
\Indec(H)
of indecomposables. ∎

This Lie coalgebra structure is closely related to the Lie algebra structure on the primitive elements.

Theorem 2.30
.

Let
H
be a graded Hopf algebra, then also
\Indec(H)
is a graded Lie coalgebra. There is an isomorphism of graded Lie algebras

\Prim(H^{\vee})\simeq\Indec(H)^{\vee}.

∎

Here
H^{\vee}
denotes the graded dual Hopf algebra of
H
and
\Indec(H)^{\vee}
denotes the graded dual Lie algebra of the Lie coalgebra
\Indec(H)
(as in Example
2.9
).

Next, we shortly explain the relationship of affine group schemes and Hopf algebras. A detailed exposition of
this interplay of algebraic geometry and abstract algebra is given in
[
18
]
and
[
41
]
. We also explain how grouplike, primitive, and indecomposable elements occur in this context.

Let
\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}
be the category of commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras with unit,
\mathsf{Sets}
be the category of sets, and
\mathsf{Groups}
be the category of groups.

Definition 2.31
.

A functor
F:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}\to\mathsf{Sets}
is an
affine scheme
if there is an object
A\in\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}
, such that
F
is naturally isomorphic to the Hom-functor

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}}(A,-):\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Sets},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}}(A,B).

In this case, one says that
A
represents the functor
F
.

Theorem 2.32
.

(Yoneda’s Lemma) Let
E,F:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}\to\mathsf{Sets}
be two affine schemes represented by
A,B
. Then any natural transformation
\Phi:E\to F
corresponds uniquely to an algebra morphism
\varphi:B\to A
. ∎

Definition 2.33
.

A functor
G:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}\to\mathsf{Groups}
is an
affine group scheme
if there is
A\in\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}
, such that
G
is naturally isomorphic to the Hom-functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}}(A,-):\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}\to\mathsf{Groups}
.

Any affine group scheme is also an affine scheme, we simply ignore the additional group structure.

Theorem 2.34
.

(
[
41
, Subsection 1.4]
) Let
F:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}\to\mathsf{Sets}
be an affine scheme represented by
A
. Then
F
is an affine group scheme if and only if
A
is a commutative Hopf algebra.
∎

Example 2.35
.

For each
R\in\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}
, consider the dual quasi-shuffle Hopf algebra
(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}})
from Theorem
2.19
. The functor

\displaystyle F:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Sets},

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\mapsto R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle

is an affine scheme represented by the commutative polynomial algebra
\mathbb{Q}[(z_{w})_{w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}}]
. The grouplike elements
\operatorname{Grp}(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle)
for the coproduct
\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}}
form a group with the concatenation product (Theorem
2.25
). Hence restricting the images of the affine scheme
F
to the grouplike elements
\operatorname{Grp}(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle)
, one obtains an affine group scheme

\displaystyle G:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Groups},

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{Grp}(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle).

The affine group scheme
G
is represented by the Hopf algebra
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond},\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})
.

Similar to the connection of Lie groups and Lie algebras, one can assign a Lie algebra functor to each affine group scheme.

Definition 2.36
.

For
R\in\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-Alg}
, let
R[\varepsilon]=R[t]/\penalty(t^{2})

be the algebra of dual numbers, so
\varepsilon^{2}=0
. For an affine group scheme
G
, the corresponding
Lie algebra functor
is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Lie}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg},

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\mapsto\ker\Big(G\big(R[\varepsilon]\twoheadrightarrow R\big)\Big).

Relating
\mathfrak{g}(R)
to the derivations on the representing Hopf algebra of
G
, which are left-invariant under the coproduct, gives the Lie algebra structure on
\mathfrak{g}(R)
.

The space
\mathfrak{g}(R)
consists of all elements in
G(R[\varepsilon])
of the form
1+\varepsilon x
. Thus, one often identifies

\mathfrak{g}(R)=\{x\mid 1+\varepsilon x\in G(R[\varepsilon])\}.

Every affine group scheme
G
is an inverse limit of algebraic affine group schemes, i.e., we have

G=\varprojlim G_{n},

where each
G_{n}
is represented by a finite dimensional Hopf algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Hence, we also have for the Lie algebra functor
\mathfrak{g}
of
G
that

\mathfrak{g}(R)=\varprojlim\mathfrak{g}_{n}(R),\qquad R\in\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}.

So,
\mathfrak{g}(R)
is a completed filtered Lie algebra, where
\operatorname{Fil}^{(j)}\mathfrak{g}(R)
constists of all elements whose projection to
\mathfrak{g}_{0}(R),\ldots,\mathfrak{g}_{j-1}(R)
is zero.

Proposition 2.37
.

Let
G
be an affine group scheme represented by a graded Hopf algebra
H
and denote by
\mathfrak{g}
the Lie algebra functor to
G
. Then one has

\operatorname{gr}\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{Q})\simeq\Indec(H)^{\vee}.

∎

By the above disscussion,
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{Q})
is filtered, hence we can apply the construction from Definition
2.6
to obtain the graded Lie algebra
\operatorname{gr}\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{Q})
.

Proposition 2.38
.

Let
G
be an affine group scheme. Then the Lie algebra functor
\mathfrak{g}:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}\to\mathsf{Lie}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}
is an affine scheme represented by
\mathcal{U}(\operatorname{gr}\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{Q}))^{\vee}
. ∎

There is an important class of affine group schemes, for which there exists a natural isomorphism to their Lie algebra functors. These group schemes are called
pro-unipotent
, a detailed discussion suitable for our context can be for example found in
[
13
, Appendix A.6]
, see also
[
10
]
.

Theorem 2.39
.

(
[
18
, IV, Proposition 4.1]
)
Let
G
be a pro-unipotent affine group scheme with Lie algebra functor
\mathfrak{g}
. Then there is a natural isomorphism

\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G.

∎

The Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series (
[
36
, p. 260]
) gives the explicit relation between the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{g}
and the group multiplication on
G
under the isomorphism
\exp
.

Example 2.40
.

In Example
2.35
we considered the dual quasi-shuffle Hopf algebra
(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}})
and obtained the corresponding affine group scheme

\displaystyle G:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Groups},

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{Grp}(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle).

The corresponding Lie algebra functor is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Lie}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg},

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\mapsto\Prim(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle),

where we mean the primitive elements for the coproduct
\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}}
. The Lie bracket is simply the commutator with respect to concatenation (cf. Theorem
2.25
).

The affine group scheme
G
is pro-unipotent. So by Theorem
2.39
, there is a natural isomorphism

\exp:\mathfrak{g}\to G.

Explicitly, this isomorphism is given by (cf. Theorem
2.27
)

\displaystyle\exp(R):\Prim(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{Grp}(R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle),

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(R)(f)=\sum_{i\geq 0}\frac{1}{i!}f^{i}.

We summarize the results from this subsection in a diagram. Let
(G,\cdot)
be a pro-unipotent affine group scheme, such that the representing Hopf algebra
(H,m_{H},\Delta_{H})
is graded, commutative and satisfies
\operatorname{rank}H_{0}=1
. Moreover, let
\mathfrak{g}
be the Lie algebra functor associated to
G
. Then there is the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H,m_{H},\Delta_{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{U}(\operatorname{gr}\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{Q})),\cdot,\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(G,\cdot)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Indec(H),\delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{gr}\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{Q}),[-,-])}
Prop
2.29
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
graded dual (
10
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1:1}
Thm
2.34
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
graded dual (Prop
2.37
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1:1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\begin{matrix}\hskip-42.67912pt\exp/\log\ +\ \operatorname{gr}\\
\text{ (Thm }\ref{exp is iso g to G})\end{matrix}}

(9)

The upper duality is obtained from Theorem
2.26
,
2.30
, and Proposition
2.37

\displaystyle H^{\vee}\simeq\mathcal{U}(\Prim(H^{\vee}))\simeq\mathcal{U}(\Indec(H)^{\vee})\simeq\mathcal{U}(\operatorname{gr}\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{Q})).

(10)

2.4
Derivations from coproducts

We fix a commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
with unit and let
(H,\ast,\mathbf{1},\Delta,\varepsilon)
be a graded Hopf algebra over
R
satisfying
\operatorname{rank}H_{0}=1
. Recall that we have

\ker(\varepsilon)=\bigoplus_{w>0}H_{w},

so the space of indecomposables (Definition
2.28
)

\Indec(H)=\ker(\varepsilon)/\ker(\varepsilon)^{2}

inherits the grading. For each positive degree
w>0
, we get a canonical projection

\displaystyle\pi_{w}:\ker(\varepsilon)\to\Indec(H)_{w}.

(11)

Definition 2.41
.

For each
w\in\mathbb{N}
define the map
D_{w}
via the composition

\textstyle{D_{w}:}
\textstyle{H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip-12.2917pt\Delta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\ker(\varepsilon)\otimes H\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip-4.09723pt\pi_{w}\otimes\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\Indec(H)_{w}\otimes H,}

where we set
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta-\mathbf{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}
.

Observe that we have by
[
35
, Proposition II.1.1]

\operatorname{im}(\Delta^{\prime})\subset\ker(\varepsilon)\otimes H=\bigoplus_{w>0}H_{w}\otimes H.

Lemma 2.42
.

For each
w\in\mathbb{N}
, the map
D_{w}\colon H\to\Indec(H)_{w}\otimes H
from Definition
2.41
is a derivation with respect to
\ast
, i.e., we have

D_{w}(u\ast v)=D_{w}(u)\ast(\mathbf{1}\otimes v)+(\mathbf{1}\otimes u)\ast D_{w}(v).

Proof.

For
u,v\in H
, we compute

\displaystyle D_{w}(u\ast v)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{w}\otimes\operatorname{id})\Big(\Delta(u\ast v)-\mathbf{1}\otimes(u\ast v)\Big)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{w}\otimes\operatorname{id})\Big(\Delta(u)\ast\Delta(v)-\mathbf{1}\otimes(u\ast v)\Big)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{w}\otimes\operatorname{id})\Big(\Delta^{\prime}(u)\ast\Delta^{\prime}(v)+(\mathbf{1}\otimes u)\ast\Delta^{\prime}(v)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt+\Delta^{\prime}(u)\ast(\mathbf{1}\otimes v)\Big)

All left tensor product factors in
\Delta^{\prime}(u)
and
\Delta^{\prime}(v)
are contained in
\ker(\varepsilon)
by construction, hence the left tensor product factors of
\Delta^{\prime}(u)\ast\Delta^{\prime}(v)
are in
\ker(\varepsilon)^{2}
. By definition of the projection
\pi_{w}
, we deduce

\displaystyle D_{w}(u\ast v)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{w}\otimes\operatorname{id})\Big((\mathbf{1}\otimes u)\ast\Delta^{\prime}(v)+\Delta^{\prime}(u)\ast(\mathbf{1}\otimes v)\Big)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}\otimes u)\ast(\pi_{w}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta^{\prime}(v)+(\pi_{w}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta^{\prime}(u)\ast(\mathbf{1}\otimes v)

\displaystyle=D_{w}(u)\ast(\mathbf{1}\otimes v)+(\mathbf{1}\otimes u)\ast D_{w}(v).

∎

Example 2.43
.

We endow the set
S=\{s_{3},s_{5},...,s_{2n+1},...\}
with the weight function given by
\operatorname{wt}(s_{w})=w
. This induces a grading on the Hopf algebra

\mathcal{U}(\Lie(S))=\big(\mathbb{Q}\langle S\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\shuffle}\big).

We write

\mathcal{U}^{\vee}=(\mathbb{Q}\langle S\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}).

for its graded dual (Example
2.9
,
2.20
). Since
\mathcal{U}^{\vee}
is a graded Hopf algebra we can study the derivations
D_{w}
of Lemma
2.42
. For simplicity, we denote

L=\Indec(\mathcal{U}^{\vee}).

Consider the element
s_{3}s_{5}s_{7}\in\mathcal{U}^{\vee}
of weight
15
. We have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}^{\prime}(s_{3}s_{5}s_{7})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}(s_{3}s_{5}s_{7})-\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{3}s_{5}s_{7}

\displaystyle=s_{3}s_{5}s_{7}\otimes\mathbf{1}+s_{3}s_{5}\otimes s_{7}+s_{3}\otimes s_{5}s_{7}\in\bigoplus_{w\geq 1}\mathcal{U}^{\vee}_{w}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\vee}.

Observe, all the left factors are Lyndon words, thus they are non zero modulo shuffle products. Hence, we get the following non-zero images in
L_{w}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\vee}

\displaystyle D_{3}(s_{3}s_{5}s_{7})

\displaystyle=\pi_{3}(s_{3})\otimes s_{5}s_{7},

\displaystyle D_{8}(s_{3}s_{5}s_{7})

\displaystyle=\pi_{8}(s_{3}s_{5})\otimes s_{7},

\displaystyle D_{15}(s_{3}s_{5}s_{7})

\displaystyle=\pi_{15}(s_{3}s_{5}s_{7})\otimes\mathbf{1}.

Definition 2.44
.

Given the Hopf algebra

\mathcal{U}^{\vee}=(\mathbb{Q}\langle S\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})
from Example
2.43

we define
\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}
by

\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}=\mathcal{U}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[s_{2}].

and we extend the deconcatenation coproduct on
\mathcal{U}^{\vee}
to a coaction

\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}:\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}\to\mathcal{U}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}

via
\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}(s_{2})=1\otimes s_{2}
.
We also set
s_{2n}=b_{n}s_{2}^{n}
, where the
b_{n}
’s are non-zero rational numbers.

We may assume that these
b_{n}
are the Bernoulli numbers given by Proposition
5.20
.
The derivation
D
extends naturally for this coaction.

Example 2.45
.

For
r\in\mathbb{N}
, we compute
D_{2r+1}(s_{2r+1}s_{2})
. First, we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}^{\prime}(s_{2r+1}s_{2})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}(s_{2r+1}s_{2})-\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{2r+1}s_{2}

\displaystyle=(s_{2r+1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{2r+1})(\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{2})-\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{2r+1}s_{2}

\displaystyle=s_{2r+1}\otimes s_{2}\in\bigoplus_{w\geq 1}\mathcal{U}^{\vee}_{w}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}.

So finally we obtain

D_{2r+1}(s_{2r+1}s_{2})=\pi_{2r+1}(s_{2r+1})\otimes s_{2}\in L_{2r+1}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}.

Definition 2.46
.

Keep the notation of Definition
2.41
.
For each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

D_{<N}=\bigoplus_{1\leq 2r+1<N}D_{2r+1}.

Example 2.47
.

If
N=2n
is even, then
\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}^{\prime}(s_{N})=0
. Indeed using
b_{n}\neq 0
we get

\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}(s_{2n})=b_{n}\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}(s_{2}^{n})=b_{n}(\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{2})^{n}=\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{2n}.

Thus
\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}^{\prime}(s_{N})=0
and also
D_{<N}(s_{N})=0
for all
N
.

Proposition 2.48
.

Let
\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}
be as in Definition
2.44

and let
D_{w}
be extended as above, then
we have for each
N\geq 2

\ker(D_{<N})\cap\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}_{N}=\mathbb{Q}\,s_{N}.

Proof.

We first show that
s_{N}
is contained in
\ker(D_{<N})
.
If
N
is even, then we have seen in Example
2.47
that
s_{N}\in\ker(D_{<N})
. If
N
is odd, then
\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}^{\prime}(s_{N})=s_{N}\otimes\mathbf{1}
and hence
\pi_{w}(s_{N})=0
for each
w<N
. Hence
D_{<N}(s_{N})=0
.

It remains to show that

\ker(D_{<N})\cap\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}_{N}\subseteq\mathbb{Q}\,s_{N}.

Let
\xi\in\ker(D_{<N})\cap\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}_{N}
and write

\xi=\alpha\,s_{N}+\sum_{3\leq 2r+1<N}s_{2r+1}u_{r}

(12)

with
\alpha\in\mathbb{Q}
and
u_{r}\in\big(\mathcal{U}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[s_{2}]\big)_{N-2r-1}
.
For
2r+1<N
, we have by definition

\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}^{\prime}(\xi)=s_{2r+1}\otimes u_{r}+\begin{pmatrix}\text{tensor products whose left factor is a }\\
\text{product of the $s_{i}$ or of weight $\neq 2r+1$}\end{pmatrix},

and hence

\displaystyle D_{2r+1}(\xi)

\displaystyle=(\pi_{2r+1}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}^{\prime}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\pi_{2r+1}(s_{2r+1})\otimes u_{r}

(13)

\displaystyle\quad+\begin{pmatrix}\text{tensor products whose left factor is a concatenation }\\
\text{ of the $s_{i}$ of weight $2r+1$ modulo shuffle products }\end{pmatrix}.

By assumption, we have
\xi\in\ker(D_{<N})
, in particular
D_{2r+1}(\xi)=0
.
It is
\pi_{2r+1}(s_{2r+1})
non zero and linear independent to all possible left factors of the second summand.
So we deduce from (
13
) that
s_{2r+1}\otimes u_{r}=0
and hence
u_{r}=0
. Since this holds for all
2r+1
, we get by (
12
) that
\xi=\alpha\,s_{N}
.
∎

3
The Ihara bracket and the Goncharov coproduct

We want to apply the general constructions from Section
2.1
to a particular setup, which arises in the context of formal and motivic multiple zeta values. We will describe the operations purely algebraically in this section and explain their occurrence in the context of formal multiple zeta values later.

3.1
Post-Lie algebras and the Grossman-Larson product

We explain the rather new and abstract theory of post-Lie algebras and their universal enveloping algebras in general. In this context, we also introduce the Grossman-Larson product. All results in this subsection are taken from
[
20
]
.
Later we use this as a convenient algebraic approach to the Ihara bracket and the Goncharov coproduct.

In the following, we fix a commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
with unit.

Definition 3.1
.

A
post-Lie algebra

(\mathfrak{g},[-,-],\triangleright)
is a Lie algebra
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-])
over
R
together with a
R
-bilinear product
\triangleright:\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
such that for all
x,y,z\in\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle x\triangleright[y,z]

\displaystyle=[x\triangleright y,z]+[y,x\triangleright z],

(14)

\displaystyle[x,y]\triangleright z

\displaystyle=x\triangleright(y\triangleright z)-(x\triangleright y)\triangleright z-y\triangleright(x\triangleright z)+(y\triangleright x)\triangleright z.

(15)

Remark 3.2
.

With the notation
L_{x}(y)=x\triangleright y
we get that (
14
) is equivalent to
L_{x}
being a Lie derivation. Equivalent to (
15
) is the identity

L_{[x,y]+L_{x}(y)+L_{y}(x)}=L_{x}\circ L_{y}-L_{y}\circ L_{x}.

It is an easy exercise to check the following proposition.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-],\triangleright)
be a post-Lie algebra. The bracket

\{x,y\}=x\triangleright y-y\triangleright x+[x,y]

(16)

satisfies the Jacobi identity for all
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
. Therefore,
\bar{\mathfrak{g}}=(\mathfrak{g},\{-,-\})
is also a Lie algebra. ∎

Definition 3.4
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},[\cdot,\cdot],\triangleright)
be a post-Lie algebra, and
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
the universal enveloping algebra of
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-])
. Let

\triangleright:\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})

be the extension of the product
\triangleright:\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{g}
recursively given by

\displaystyle x\triangleright 1=0

(17)

\displaystyle 1\triangleright A=A

(18)

\displaystyle xA\triangleright y=x\triangleright(A\triangleright y)-(x\triangleright A)\triangleright y

(19)

\displaystyle A\triangleright BC=(A_{(1)}\triangleright B)(A_{(2)}\triangleright C).

(20)

for all
A,B,C\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
and
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
. Here, we use Sweedler’s notation for the coproduct on
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
:

\Delta(A)=A_{(1)}\otimes A_{(2)}.

A simple application of (
18
) and (
20
) leads to the following.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-],\triangleright)
be a post-Lie algebra. We have for
x,t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathfrak{g}

x\triangleright(t_{1}\cdots t_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{1}\cdots t_{i-1}(x\triangleright t_{i})t_{i+1}\cdots t_{n}.

Proposition 3.6
.

(
[
20
, Proposition 3.1]
) Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-],\triangleright)
be a post-Lie algebra. Then the product
\triangleright:\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})\times\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})\to\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
given in Definition
3.4
is well-defined and unique. ∎

To prove this, use Lemma
3.5
together with
(
20
) and induction on the length of
B
to extend
A\triangleright B
to monomials
A
and
B
. For more details we refer to
[
20
]
and in particular to
[
37
, Proposition 2.7]
.

Definition 3.7
.

Let
(\mathfrak{g},[-,-],\triangleright)
be a post-Lie algebra. On the universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
the
Grossman-Larson product
is defined by

A\circledast B=A_{(1)}(A_{(2)}\triangleright B).

If
x\in\mathfrak{g}
, then for all
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
we get

x\circledast A=x\triangleright A+xA
. In particular, for
y\in\mathfrak{g}
we recover the Lie bracket of Proposition
3.3
via

x\circledast y-y\circledast x=x\triangleright y-y\triangleright x+[x,y]=\{x,y\}.

For any grouplike element
G\in\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{g}))
we get for any
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
the formula

G\circledast A=G(G\triangleright A).

Central for the application we have in mind is the following theorem.

Theorem 3.8
.

(
[
20
, Theorem 3.4]
) The Grossman-Larson product is associative and defines on
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
also the structure of
an associative Hopf algebra
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}),\circledast,\Delta)
.
Moreover, this Hopf algebra is isomorphic to the enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}(\bar{\mathfrak{g}})
. ∎

For the proof, first observe that the Grossman-Larson product
\circledast
preserves the filtration on
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
given by the length of the monomials. The associated graded to the Grossman-Larson product with respect to this filtration is simply concatenation. So we obtain an isomorphism of graded Hopf algebras
\operatorname{gr}\mathcal{U}(\bar{\mathfrak{g}})\to\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
, which results in a Hopf algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{U}(\bar{\mathfrak{g}})\to(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}),\circledast,\Delta)
. For a detailed proof in the analogue commutative setup we refer to
[
37
, Theorem 2.12]
.

The antipode
S
of the Hopf algebra
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}),\circledast,\Delta)
differs from the standard antipode on the universal enveloping algebra
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
. It can be computed recursively from

\displaystyle\circledast\circ(S\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta=\eta\circ\varepsilon,

(21)

where
\eta
is the usual unit map und
\varepsilon
the usual counit map of
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
.

Example 3.9
.

For
x,y\in\mathfrak{g}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\circledast\circ(S\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta(xy)

\displaystyle=S(xy)\circledast 1+S(x)\circledast y+S(y)\circledast x+S(1)\circledast xy

\displaystyle=S(xy)-x\circledast y-y\circledast x+xy

\displaystyle=S(xy)-x\triangleright y-xy-y\triangleright x-yx+xy

\displaystyle=S(xy)-x\triangleright y-y\triangleright x-yx.

Since we have
\eta(\varepsilon(xy))=0
, we deduce from (
21
)

\displaystyle S(xy)=x\triangleright y+y\triangleright x+yx.

The standard antipode on
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
is just given by
xy\mapsto yx
.

Similar computations show that we have for
x,y,z\in\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle S(xyz)=\

\displaystyle-zyx-x\triangleright zy-y\triangleright zx-z\triangleright yx+[x,y]\triangleright z+[x,z]\triangleright y+[y,z]\triangleright x

\displaystyle-x\triangleright(z\triangleright y)-x\triangleright(y\triangleright z)-(y\triangleright x)\triangleright z-y\triangleright(z\triangleright x)-(z\triangleright x)\triangleright y

\displaystyle-(z\triangleright y)\triangleright x

and the standard antipode on
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g})
is just
xyz\mapsto-zyx
.

These examples show that a closed formula for the antipode
S
is not at all obvious.

3.2
The Ihara bracket

We introduce the Ihara bracket and explain it in the context of post-Lie algebras. In the following, let
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
be the free Lie algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
on the alphabet
\mathcal{X}=\{x_{0},x_{1}\}
, and we denote its Lie bracket by
[-,-]
.

Definition 3.10
.

For each
f\in\mathfrak{f}_{2}
a special derivation is defined by

d_{f}(x_{i})=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{ if }i=0,\\
[x_{1},f]&\mbox{ if }i=1.\end{cases}

Let
\triangleright:\mathfrak{f}_{2}\times\mathfrak{f}_{2}\to\mathfrak{f}_{2}
be the bilinear product given by

f\triangleright g=d_{f}(g).

Proposition 3.11
.

The tuple
(\mathfrak{f}_{2},[-,-],\triangleright)
is a post-Lie algebra.

Proof.

By construction (
14
) is satisfied. The equation in (
15
) is equivalent to

d_{d_{f}(g)-d_{g}(f)+[f,g]}=d_{f}\circ d_{g}-d_{g}\circ d_{f},\qquad f,g\in\mathfrak{f}_{2}.

On both sides are derivations, hence it suffices to check the equation on the generators
x_{0},x_{1}
. For the first case, we directly obtain

d_{d_{f}(g)-d_{g}(f)+[f,g]}(x_{0})=0=(d_{f}\circ d_{g}-d_{g}\circ d_{f})(x_{0}).

For the second case, we compute

\displaystyle(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}_{f}\circ d_{g}-d_{g}\circ d_{f})(x_{1})

\displaystyle=d_{f}([x_{1},g])-d_{g}([x_{1},f])

\displaystyle=[x_{1},d_{f}(g)]+[d_{f}(x_{1}),g]-[x_{1},d_{g}(f)]-[d_{g}(x_{1}),f]

\displaystyle=[x_{1},d_{f}(g)]-[x_{1},d_{g}(f)]+[[x_{1},f],g]-[[x_{1},g],f]

\displaystyle=[x_{1},d_{f}(g)]-[x_{1},d_{g}(f)]+[x_{1},[f,g]]

\displaystyle=d_{d_{f}(g)-d_{g}(f)+[f,g]}(x_{1}),

where the fourth step follows from the Jacobi identity for
[-,-]
.
∎

Definition 3.12
.

The
Ihara bracket
on
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
is defined as

\{f,g\}=d_{f}(g)-d_{g}(f)+[f,g].

An immediate consequence of Proposition
3.3
is the following.

Corollary 3.13
.

The pair
(\mathfrak{f}_{2},\{-,-\})
is a Lie algebra.

3.3
The Grossman-Larson product for the Ihara bracket

We now study on the universal enveloping algebra

\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})=(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\shuffle})

the Grossman-Larson product (Definition
3.7
) determined by the Ihara bracket. We start with some formulas, which simplify the calculation of the extended product
\triangleright
.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. For all
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathfrak{f}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})\triangleright x_{i}=\begin{cases}0&\quad\text{if}\quad i=0,\\
[\cdots[[x_{1},a_{1}],a_{2}],\ldots,a_{n}]&\quad\text{if}\quad i=1.\end{cases}

Proof.

For
n=1
, the claim follows from the definition of
\triangleright
. We prove the claim by induction on the number
n
of Lie elements. By (
19
) and Lemma
3.5
, we have

\displaystyle(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})\triangleright x_{0}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\triangleright\big((a_{2}\cdots a_{n})\triangleright x_{0}\big)-\big(a_{1}\triangleright(a_{2}\cdots a_{n})\big)\triangleright x_{0}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\triangleright 0-\big(a_{1}\triangleright(a_{2}\cdots a_{n})\big)\triangleright x_{0}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=2}^{n}\Big(a_{2}\cdots a_{i-1}(a_{1}\triangleright a_{i})a_{i+1}\cdots a_{n}\Big)\triangleright x_{0}

\displaystyle=0.

The last step follows from the induction hypotheses, since
a_{1}\triangleright a_{i}
is also a Lie element. Similarly, if
a_{2}\neq x_{1}
we compute

\displaystyle(a_{1}\cdots a_{n})\triangleright x_{1}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\triangleright\big((a_{2}\cdots a_{n})\triangleright x_{1}\big)-\big(a_{1}\triangleright(a_{2}\cdots a_{n})\big)\triangleright x_{1}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\triangleright[\cdots[[x_{1},a_{2}],a_{3}],\ldots,a_{n}]-\big(a_{1}\triangleright(a_{2}\cdots a_{n})\big)\triangleright x_{1}

\displaystyle=[\cdots[[x_{1},a_{1}],a_{2}],\ldots,a_{n}]

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\Big([\cdots[[x_{1},a_{2}],a_{3}],\ldots,a_{1}\triangleright a_{i}],\ldots,a_{n}]

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt-\big(a_{2}a_{3}\cdots(a_{1}\triangleright a_{i})\cdots a_{n}\big)\triangleright x_{1}\Big)

\displaystyle=[\cdots[[x_{1},a_{1}],a_{2}],\ldots,a_{n}]

\displaystyle+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\Big([\cdots[[x_{1},a_{2}],a_{3}],\ldots,a_{1}\triangleright a_{i}],\ldots,a_{n}]

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt-[\cdots[[x_{1},a_{2}],a_{3}],\ldots,a_{1}\triangleright a_{i}],\ldots,a_{n}]\Big)

\displaystyle=[\cdots[[x_{1},a_{1}],a_{2}],\ldots,a_{n}].

For
a_{2}=x_{1}
the claim follows by a small modification of the above calculations.
∎

Proposition 3.15
.

For
A,B\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})
, we have

A\triangleright x_{0}B=x_{0}(A\triangleright B).

Proof.

By (
20
), we have

A\triangleright x_{0}B=(A_{(1)}\triangleright x_{0})(A_{(2)}\triangleright B).

Since
A_{(1)}
consists of (possibly empty) products of elements in
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
, we deduce from Proposition
3.14
that
(A_{(1)}\triangleright x_{0})
vanishes unless
A_{(1)}
corresponds to the empty word (cf. (
18
)). So the summand
\mathbf{1}\otimes A
is the only one in
\Delta(A)
with a non-zero contribution. Hence

(A_{(1)}\triangleright x_{0})(A_{(2)}\triangleright B)=(\mathbf{1}\triangleright x_{0})(A\triangleright B)=x_{0}(A\triangleright B).\qed

Proposition 3.16
.

For
A,B\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})
, we have

x_{1}A\triangleright B=0.

Proof.

Write
B=b_{1}\cdots b_{m}
where
b_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
.
We prove the claim via induction on
\operatorname{dep}(B)=\#\{i\mid b_{i}=x_{1}\}
.
If
B=x_{0}^{m}
then repeatedly applying Proposition
3.15
yields

x_{1}A\triangleright B=x_{0}^{m-1}(x_{1}A\triangleright x_{0})=0,

where the last equality follows from Proposition
3.14
.
Now assume
\operatorname{dep}(B)=M\geq 1
, and let
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
denote the smallest integer such that
b_{j}=x_{1}
. By applying Proposition
3.15

j-1
times we obtain that

x_{1}A\triangleright B=x_{0}^{j-1}(x_{1}A\triangleright x_{1}b_{j+1}\cdots b_{m})\overset{\eqref{Ext3}}{=}x_{0}^{j-1}\big((x_{1}A)_{(1)}\triangleright x_{1}\big)\big((x_{1}A)_{(2)}\triangleright b_{j+1}\cdots b_{m}\big).

Since
\Delta(x_{1}A)=(x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{1})\Delta(A)
it is clear that each tensor product in
\Delta(x_{1}A)
has at least one factor starting in
x_{1}
. If the left factor starts in
x_{1}
, then
\big((x_{1}A)_{(1)}\triangleright x_{1}\big)=0
by Proposition
3.14
since
[x_{1},x_{1}]=0
. If the right factor starts in
x_{1}
then
\big((x_{1}A)_{(2)}\triangleright b_{j+1}\cdots b_{m}\big)=0
by induction hypothesis since
\operatorname{dep}(b_{j+1}\cdots b_{m})=M-1
.
∎

Example 3.17
.

We calculate
x_{0}x_{0}\circledast x_{0}x_{1}
. At first we observe

\displaystyle\Delta_{\shuffle}(x_{0}x_{0})=x_{0}x_{0}\otimes\mathbf{1}+2x_{0}\otimes x_{0}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{0},

and thus by Definition
3.7
we need to determine

\displaystyle x_{0}x_{0}\circledast x_{0}x_{1}

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{0}(\mathbf{1}\triangleright x_{0}x_{1})+2x_{0}(x_{0}\triangleright x_{0}x_{1})+\mathbf{1}(x_{0}x_{0}\triangleright x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}+2x_{0}(x_{0}\triangleright x_{0}x_{1})+x_{0}x_{0}\triangleright x_{0}x_{1}.

For the third term, we obtain from Propositions
3.15
and
3.14

\displaystyle x_{0}x_{0}\triangleright x_{0}x_{1}=x_{0}(x_{0}x_{0}\triangleright x_{1})=x_{0}[[x_{1},x_{0}],x_{0}].

Similarly, one computes for the second term

\displaystyle x_{0}\triangleright x_{0}x_{1}=x_{0}(x_{0}\triangleright x_{1})=x_{0}[x_{1},x_{0}].

Altogether, we get

\displaystyle x_{0}x_{0}\circledast x_{0}x_{1}

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}+2x_{0}x_{0}[x_{1},x_{0}]+x_{0}[[x_{1},x_{0}],x_{0}]

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{1}x_{0}x_{0}.

We want to give a closed formula for the Grossman-Larson product corresponding to the Ihara bracket.

Recall the
j
-th letter function from Notation
2.11
, i. e.

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{j}:\mathcal{X}^{*}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{X}\cup\{\mathbf{1}\},

\displaystyle x_{i_{1}}\cdots x_{i_{n}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}x_{i_{j}}\quad&\text{ if }j\leq n,\\
\mathbf{1}&\text{ else}.\end{cases}

Instead of
w=\varepsilon_{1}(w)\varepsilon_{2}(w)\cdots\varepsilon_{n}(w)\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, we will often just write
w=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}
where
n
is the
weight
, i.e., the number of letters, of
w
.
The antipode
S
of the Hopf algebra
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\shuffle})
is given by

\displaystyle S:\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),

(22)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}

\displaystyle\mapsto(-1)^{n}\varepsilon_{n}\cdots\varepsilon_{1}.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})
and all words
w=x_{0}^{k_{1}}x_{1}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}}x_{1}x_{0}^{k_{d+1}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})
, we have

\displaystyle A\circledast w=A_{(1)}x_{0}^{k_{1}}S(A_{(2)})x_{1}A_{(3)}x_{0}^{k_{2}}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}}S(A_{(2d)})x_{1}A_{(2d+1)}x_{0}^{k_{d+1}}.

Here, we use the iterated Sweedler notation

\Delta_{\shuffle}^{n}(A)=A_{(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes A_{(n+1)},\qquad n\geq 1.

Proof.

For any
A\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})
and any word
w=x_{0}^{k_{1}}x_{1}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}}x_{1}x_{0}^{k_{d+1}}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})
, we obtain from Definition
3.7
and several applications of (
20
) that

\displaystyle A\circledast w

\displaystyle=A_{(1)}(A_{(2)}\triangleright w)=A_{(1)}(A_{(2)}\triangleright x_{0}^{k_{1}}x_{1})\cdots(A_{(d+1)}\triangleright x_{0}^{k_{d}}x_{1})(A_{(d+2)}\triangleright x_{0}^{k_{d+1}})

From Proposition
3.15
, we deduce that

\displaystyle A\circledast w

\displaystyle=A_{(1)}x_{0}^{k_{1}}(A_{(2)}\triangleright x_{1})\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}}(A_{(d+1)}\triangleright x_{1})x_{0}^{k_{d+1}}.

(23)

By Proposition
3.14
and the definition of the coproduct
\Delta_{\shuffle}
(Example
2.20
) and the antipode
S
(see (
22
)), we have for
A=a_{1}\cdots a_{n}\in\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})

\displaystyle A\triangleright x_{1}=[\cdots[[x_{1},a_{1}],a_{2}],\ldots,a_{n}]=S(A_{(1)})x_{1}A_{(2)}.

(24)

Combining (
23
) and (
24
) gives the claimed formula

\displaystyle A\circledast w=A_{(1)}x_{0}^{k_{1}}S(A_{(2)})x_{1}A_{(3)}x_{0}^{k_{2}}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}}S(A_{(2d)})x_{1}A_{(2d+1)}x_{0}^{k_{d+1}}.

∎

From Theorem
3.8
, we obtain the following.

Theorem 3.19
.

The tuple
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),\circledast,\Delta_{\shuffle})
is a cocommutative Hopf algebra.

This result was also stated in by Willwacher
[
42
, Proposition 7.1]
, but proved completely differently.

3.4
The Goncharov coproduct

The aim of this subsection is to complete the diagram (
9
) for the Ihara bracket. In the last subsection, we studied the universal enveloping algebra
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),\circledast,\Delta_{\shuffle})
of
(\mathfrak{f}_{2},\{-,-\})
. So the next step is to consider the grouplike elements of
(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),\circledast,\Delta_{\shuffle})
. In order to get a non-empty set, we have to pass to the completed Hopf algebra
(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),\circledast,\Delta_{\shuffle})
in the sense of Definition
2.5
. So precisely, consider the set

\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))=\{A\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\rangle\mid\Delta_{\shuffle}(A)=A\otimes A\}.

From Corollary
3.19
and Theorem
2.25
we deduce the following.

Corollary 3.20
.

The pair
(\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})),\circledast)
is a group.

Remark 3.21
.

By Theorem
2.25
, the grouplike elements
G\in\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))
satisfy

S(G)=G^{-1},

where the inverse is meant with respect to concatenation
2
2

2

In fact, the triple
(\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),\operatorname{conc},\circledast)
is a post-group. This is a general phenomenon for any post-Lie algebra: the corresponding set of group like elements in the completed enveloping algebra is endowed with two group structures: one for the ”ordinary” product given by concatenation, the other for the Grossman Larson product. These product structures interact nicely. For an introduction to post-groups, see

[
7
]

and
[
2
]

.
Hence we have for all
G,H\in\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))

G\circledast H=G\kappa_{G}(H),

where
\kappa_{G}
is the automorphism on
\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})
with respect to concatenation given by

\kappa_{G}(x_{i})=\begin{cases}x_{0}&\mbox{ if }\,i=0,\\
G^{-1}x_{1}G&\mbox{ if }\,i=1.\end{cases}

This formula equals the one given in
[
38
]
.

Remark 3.22
.

Viewing
(\mathfrak{f}_{2},\{-,-\})
as a post-Lie algebra an applying the construction of the Grossman-Larson product gives a very direct and explicit way to derive the group multiplication on
\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))
from the Lie bracket
\{-,-\}
on
\mathfrak{f}_{2}
. So the Grossman-Larson product can be seen as the reverse construction of the usual linearization of the group multiplication to obtain the Lie bracket (cf
[
38
, Section II.2.2]
).

For the last step of completing the diagram (
9
), we view the grouplike elements as an affine group scheme

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{Groups},

(25)

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\mapsto(\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}\otimes R)),\circledast).

Then following Example
2.35
, this affine scheme is represented by the algebra
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)
. We want to describe the coproduct on
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)
induced by the multiplication
\circledast
on the affine group scheme (cf Theorem
2.34
). We follow the calculations given in
[
10
, Subsection 3.10.6.]
in order to obtain the explicit formula for this coproduct.

Notation 3.23
.

For
\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b}\in\mathcal{X}
and any
f\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\rangle
, we set

I(\varepsilon_{a};f;\varepsilon_{b})=\begin{cases}f,&(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b})=(x_{1},x_{0}),\\
S(f),&(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b})=(x_{0},x_{1}),\\
(f\mid\mathbf{1})\cdot\mathbf{1},&\varepsilon_{a}=\varepsilon_{b}.\end{cases}

Definition 3.24
.

(
[
26
]
)
Let
\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, then the
Goncharov coproduct
is given by the formula

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq n\\
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}<n+1\end{subarray}}\ \Bigshuffle_{p=0}^{k}I(\varepsilon_{i_{p}};\varepsilon_{i_{p}+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}-1};\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}})\otimes\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}},

(26)

where we set
i_{0}=0,\,i_{k+1}=n+1
and
\varepsilon_{0}=x_{1},\,\varepsilon_{n+1}=x_{0}
.

For any
f\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\rangle
with constant term
0
we have that
I(\varepsilon;f;\varepsilon)=0
for any
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{X}
(Notation
3.23
). Hence, there are usually a lot of vanishing terms in the Goncharov coproduct. We give examples and graphical interpretations for this coproduct in Subsection
3.5
.

The following lemma is a reformulation of
[
10
, Proposition 3.422]
.

Lemma 3.25
.

For all
G,H\in\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))
, and
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
, we have the identity

(G\circledast H\mid w)=(G\otimes H\mid\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(w)),

where the duality pairing
(\cdot\mid\cdot)
is given in (
5
).

The equality holds also for an arbitrary element
H\in\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})
,
H
being grouplike is not used in the proof. But in everything what follows, we only need the result as stated above.

Proof.

We follow the calculations given in
[
10
, Subsection 3.10.6.]
. For
G\in\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))
, we have by Theorem
2.25

I(\varepsilon_{0};G;\varepsilon_{n+1})=\begin{cases}G,&(\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{n+1})=(x_{1},x_{0}),\\
G^{-1},&(\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{n+1})=(x_{0},x_{1}),\\
\mathbf{1},&\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{n+1}.\end{cases}

Let
G\in\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))
and
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
. By Theorem
3.18
, the product
G\circledast w
can be computed as follows:

•

if
w
starts with
x_{0}
, put
G
at the beginning,

•

between every
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
in
w
, insert
G^{-1}
,

•

between every
x_{1}
and
x_{0}
in
w
, insert
G
,

•

if
w
ends with
x_{1}
, put
G
at the end.

Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle G\circledast(\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n})=I(x_{1};G;\varepsilon_{1})\varepsilon_{1}I(\varepsilon_{1};G;\varepsilon_{2})\varepsilon_{2}\ldots\varepsilon_{n-1}I(\varepsilon_{n-1};G;\varepsilon_{n})\varepsilon_{n}I(\varepsilon_{n};G;x_{0}).

For
G,H\in\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))
with

H=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{X}^{*}}h(w)\varepsilon_{1}(w)\cdots\varepsilon_{\operatorname{wt}(w)}(w),\qquad\varepsilon_{i}(w)\in\mathcal{X},

and
\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, we compute

\displaystyle(G\circledast H

\displaystyle\mid\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{X}^{*}}h(w)\Big(I\big(x_{1};G;\varepsilon_{1}(w)\big)\varepsilon_{1}(w)I\big(\varepsilon_{1}(w);G;\varepsilon_{2}(w)\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\cdots\varepsilon_{\operatorname{wt}(w)}(w)I\big(\varepsilon_{\operatorname{wt}(w)}(w);G;x_{0}\big)\ \Big|\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq n\\
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}<n+1\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=0}^{k}\Big(I\big(\xi_{i_{p}};G;\xi_{i_{p+1}}\big)\ \Big|\ \xi_{i_{p}+1}\cdots\xi_{i_{p+1}-1}\Big)\Big(H\ \Big|\ \xi_{i_{1}}\cdots\xi_{i_{k}}\Big).

Since
I(\xi_{a};-;\xi_{b})
is either an involution or constant, we deduce

\displaystyle(G\circledast H

\displaystyle\mid\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq n\\
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}<n+1\end{subarray}}\prod_{p=0}^{k}\Big(G\ \Big|\ I\big(\xi_{i_{p}};\xi_{i_{p}+1},\ldots,\xi_{i_{p+1}-1};\xi_{i_{p+1}}\big)\Big)\Big(H\ \Big|\ \xi_{i_{1}}\cdots\xi_{i_{k}}\Big).

Any grouplike element
G
for
\Delta_{\shuffle}
satisfies by duality

(G\mid w_{1})(G\mid w_{2})=(G\mid w_{1}\ \shuffle\ w_{2})

for all
w_{1},w_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle(G\circledast H

\displaystyle\mid\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq n\\
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}<n+1\end{subarray}}\Big(G\ \Big|\ \Bigshuffle_{p=0}^{k}I\big(\xi_{i_{p}};\xi_{i_{p}+1},\ldots,\xi_{i_{p+1}-1};\xi_{i_{p+1}}\big)\Big)\Big(H\ \Big|\ \xi_{i_{1}}\cdots\xi_{i_{k}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq n\\
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}<n+1\end{subarray}}\Big(G\otimes H\ \Big|\ \Bigshuffle_{p=0}^{k}I\big(\xi_{i_{p}};\xi_{i_{p}+1},\ldots,\xi_{i_{p+1}-1};\xi_{i_{p+1}}\big)\otimes\xi_{i_{1}}\cdots\xi_{i_{k}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(G\otimes H\ \big|\ \Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})\big),

where
\xi_{i_{0}}=x_{1}
and
\xi_{i_{k+1}}=x_{0}
.
∎

Note that for any two words
u,v\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
we can find elements
G,H\in\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))
such that
(G\otimes H\,|\,u\otimes v)\neq 0
. Thus, Lemma
3.25
uniquely determines the coproduct on
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
dual to
\circledast
with respect to the pairing
(\cdot|\cdot)
.

Since
R\mapsto(\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}\otimes R)),\circledast)
is an affine scheme represented by the algebra
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)
, and by Lemma
3.25
the multiplication
\circledast
corresponds to the Goncharov coproduct on
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)
, we get the following.

Proposition 3.26
.

The tuple
