(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}})
is a weight-graded Hopf algebra.

Summarizing the previous results gives the following diagram (cf (
9
))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),\circledast,\Delta_{\shuffle})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2})),\circledast)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Indec(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle),\delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathfrak{f}_{2},\{-,-\})}
mod products
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
graded dual
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1:1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
graded dual
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1:1}

(27)

The bijective exponential map
\exp:\widehat{\mathfrak{f}_{2}}\to\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}))
is given in Example
2.40
. By Theorem
3.8
, this exponential map can also be used in this case.

3.5
Combinatorics of the Goncharov coproduct

Recall from Definition
3.24
that we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq n\\
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}<n+1\end{subarray}}\ \Bigshuffle_{p=0}^{k}I(\varepsilon_{i_{p}};\varepsilon_{i_{p}+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}-1};\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}})\otimes\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}},

where
\varepsilon_{1}\dots\varepsilon_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
,
i_{0}=0,\,i_{k+1}=n+1
and
\varepsilon_{0}=x_{1},\,\varepsilon_{n+1}=x_{0}
.

We start by expressing the Goncharov coproduct in a more combinatorial way.

Notation 3.27
.

Let
w=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
be a word of weight
n
. Then any subset
I=\{i_{1},...,i_{k}\}
of
I_{n}=\{1,...,n\}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
defines a word

\displaystyle v_{I}=\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}.

(28)

So we express the Goncharov coproduct as

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(w)=\sum_{I\subset I_{n}}P_{I}(w)\otimes v_{I},

(29)

where

\displaystyle P_{I}(w)=\Bigshuffle_{p=0}^{k}I(\varepsilon_{i_{p}};u_{p};\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}}).

(30)

Here we set
i_{0}=0
,
i_{k+1}=n+1
,
\varepsilon_{0}=x_{1}
,
\varepsilon_{n+1}=x_{0}
, and
u_{p}=\varepsilon_{i_{p}+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}-1}
.
In the formula (
29
) we refer to the words
v_{I}
as
subwords

and to the
u_{p}
in (
30
) as
quotient words
. Moreover, we call
\varepsilon_{i_{p}}u_{p}\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}}
the
enlarged subword
associated to
u_{p}
.

We explain now two ways to give an graphical interpretation of this formula.

On the one hand, there is the graphical interpretation introduced by Goncharov in
[
26
]
. Given a word
\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, one locates the letters
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
on the upper half of a semicircle and adds
x_{1}
and
x_{0}
at the left- and right-hand side, respectively. Then the term in the formula from Definition
3.24
that corresponds to the subword
v=\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}
is depicted by a polygon inscribed into the semicircle with vertices
x_{1},\varepsilon_{i_{1}},\dots,\varepsilon_{i_{k}},x_{0}
.

For example the subword
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{3}\varepsilon_{4}\varepsilon_{7}\varepsilon_{n}}
corresponds to the summand

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}I(\varepsilon_{0};\varepsilon_{1};\varepsilon_{2})I(\varepsilon_{2};\mathbf{1};\varepsilon_{3})I(\varepsilon_{3};\mathbf{1};\varepsilon_{4})I(\varepsilon_{4};\varepsilon_{5}\varepsilon_{6};\varepsilon_{7})I(\varepsilon_{7};\varepsilon_{8}\dots\varepsilon_{n-1};\varepsilon_{n})I(\varepsilon_{n};\mathbf{1};\varepsilon_{n+1})}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{3}\varepsilon_{4}\varepsilon_{7}\varepsilon_{n}}

and is depicted by

x_{1}
\varepsilon_{2}
\varepsilon_{3}
\varepsilon_{4}
\varepsilon_{7}
\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{5}
\varepsilon_{6}
\varepsilon_{8}
\dots
\varepsilon_{n-2}
\varepsilon_{n-1}
\varepsilon_{n}
x_{0}

Figure 1:
Goncharov’s semicircle

On the other hand, the left hand factors in a summand of the Goncharov
coproduct corresponds to a choice of distinct strict subwords
u_{p}
of
\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}
(cf Notation
2.11
). For example, the choice of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{1}}
,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{5}\varepsilon_{6}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\varepsilon_{8}\cdots\varepsilon_{n-2}\varepsilon_{n-1}}
in the word
\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}

corresponds to the above summand in its reduced form, i.e., the trivial factors are omitted,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}I(\varepsilon_{0};\varepsilon_{1};\varepsilon_{2})I(\varepsilon_{4};\varepsilon_{5}\varepsilon_{6};\varepsilon_{7})I(\varepsilon_{7};\varepsilon_{8}\dots\varepsilon_{n-1};\varepsilon_{n})}\otimes{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{3}\varepsilon_{4}\varepsilon_{7}\varepsilon_{n}}.

This can be visualized by
eating worms
as follows:

Figure 2:
”Eating worms”

We refer to the red and blue parts to ”red worms” and ”blue worms”, respectively. The ”red worms” have open mouths towards its boundaries which are either a ”blue worm” or one of the outer boundaries
x_{1}
and
x_{0}
.

Example 3.28
.

We consider
x_{0}^{n-1}x_{1}\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
for
n\in\{1,2,3,4\}
.
For
n=1
we compute directly that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{1})=I(x_{1};x_{1};x_{0})\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{1}=x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{1}.

The summands for
n=2
are depicted via Goncharov’s semicircle (cf. Figure
1
) as follows
x_{1}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{0}

Thus we get

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=I(x_{1};x_{0},x_{1};x_{0})\otimes\mathbf{1}+I(x_{1};\mathbf{1};x_{0})I(x_{0};x_{1};x_{0})\otimes x_{0}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38092pt+I(x_{1};x_{0};x_{1})I(x_{1};\mathbf{1};x_{0})\otimes x_{1}+I(x_{1};\mathbf{1};x_{0})\otimes x_{0}x_{1}

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{1}.

The summands for
n=3
are visualized as ”eating worms” as follows:

Observe that half of the summands vanish by the convention given in Notation
3.23
as at least one ”red worm” starts and ends in the same letter. We thus have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}-x_{0}\otimes x_{0}x_{1}+x_{0}\otimes x_{0}x_{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}.

So
x_{0}^{n-1}x_{1}
is primitive for
n=1,2,3
.
However, for
n=4
one similarly computes that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}-2x_{0}x_{0}\otimes x_{0}x_{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}.

Example 3.29
.

We want to show the compatibility of
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}
and
\shuffle
in a small example. It is easy to see that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{i})

\displaystyle=x_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{i}\qquad(i=0,1),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{1},

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{1}x_{0})

\displaystyle=x_{1}x_{0}\otimes\mathbf{1}+x_{0}\otimes x_{1}+x_{1}\otimes x_{0}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{1}x_{0}.

Now we compute

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}\shuffle x_{1})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{1}+x_{1}x_{0})

\displaystyle=(x_{0}x_{1}+x_{1}x_{0})\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes(x_{0}x_{1}+x_{1}x_{0})+x_{0}\otimes x_{1}+x_{1}\otimes x_{0}

\displaystyle=(x_{0}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0})\shuffle(x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{1})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0})\shuffle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{1}).

3.6
The derivations
D_{w}
for the Goncharov coproduct

We make the derivations
D_{w}
, which we introduced in Subsection
2.4
in a general context, explicit for the Goncharov coproduct.

For simplicitly and to continue Brown’s notation, we abbreviate

\mathcal{L}=\Indec(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle).

The weight, i.e., the number of letters, defines a grading on
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)
and hence also on the space
\mathcal{L}
of indecomposables. We denote the homogeneous subspaces of weight
w
by
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle_{w}
resp.
\mathcal{L}_{w}
. For each odd weight
2r+1
,
r\geq 1
, the derivation
D_{2r+1}
is given by

\textstyle{D_{2r+1}:}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip-24.58342pt\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{w\geq 1}\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle_{w}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hskip 4.09723pt\pi_{2r+1}\otimes\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{2r+1}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle.}

Recall that
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}^{\prime}=\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}-\mathbf{1}\otimes\operatorname{id}
and
\pi_{2r+1}:\bigoplus_{w\geq 1}\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle_{w}\to\mathcal{L}_{2r+1}
is the canonical projection.

Proposition 3.30
.

Let
w=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, then each summand in
D_{2r+1}(w)
corresponds to exactly one strict subword
u=\varepsilon_{j+1}\dots\varepsilon_{j+2r+1}
with
0\leq j\leq n-2r-1
of
w
.

Proof.

For each non-strict subword, the left tensor factor in the Goncharov coproduct (
26
) becomes a non-trivial product, and thus vanishes under the projection
\pi_{2r+1}
.
∎

Remark 3.31
.

Following the visualization of
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}
on a semicircle from Figure
1
, we can depict the map
D_{2r+1}
by omitting trivial factors of the following type
x_{1}
x_{0}
\varepsilon_{1}
\varepsilon_{j-1}
\varepsilon_{j}
\varepsilon_{j+1}
\varepsilon_{j+2r}
\varepsilon_{j+2r+1}
\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}
\varepsilon_{n}
\dots
\dots
\dots

4
The space
\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
and level lowering inspired by
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}

The subspace
\mathcal{B}_{2,3}\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
of words in
\mathsf{2}
and
\mathsf{3}
has a natural level filtration given by the number of occurences of the letter
\mathsf{3}
. This subspace behaves nicely with respect to the Goncharov coproduct and
we will show in this section that a variant of the derivations
D_{2r+1}
made out of the Goncharov coproduct give rise to level lowering maps.

4.1
Level filtration and level lowering maps

Definition 4.1
.

We set

\mathcal{B}_{2,3}=\mathbb{Q}\langle x_{0}x_{1},\,x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}\rangle\subset\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle.

The
level

\deg_{3}(w)
of a word
w\in\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
is given by the number of
x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}
in
w
. This induces an ascending
level filtration
on
\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
given by

F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\Big\{w\in\mathcal{B}_{2,3}\;\Big|\;\mbox{number of }x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}\mbox{ in }w\leq\ell\Big\}

for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

Definition 4.2
.

Consider the alphabet
\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}
and let
\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}^{*}
the set of all words with letters in
\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}
. Define the map

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}\colon\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}^{*}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathcal{X}^{*}

by
\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{2})=x_{0}x_{1}
and
\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{3})=x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}
and extend this with respect to concatenation.

Obviously, the image
\mathsf{b}\big(\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}^{*}\big)\subset\mathcal{X}^{*}
gives a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis for
\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
.

The
weight
of a word
u\in\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}^{*}
is given by
\operatorname{wt}(u)=\operatorname{wt}\big(\mathsf{b}(u)\big)
, and similarly, the
level
of
u\in\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}^{*}
is given by
\deg_{3}(u)=\deg_{3}(\mathsf{b}(u))
.

Lemma 4.3
.

We have

\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}\colon\mathcal{B}_{2,3}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\otimes\mathcal{B}_{2,3}.

Proof.

Let
w=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
be a word.
It suffices to show, if
v=\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}
is subword of
w
, which determines a non-trivial contribution in the Goncharov coproduct (
26
), then
v\in\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
.
It is
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}=x_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=x_{1}
, since otherwise the factors

I(x_{1};\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{1}-1};\varepsilon_{i_{1}})
and
I(\varepsilon_{i_{k}};\varepsilon_{i_{k}+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n};x_{0})
vanish.
If
x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}
is a strict subword of
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}
, then there is a vanishing factor
I(x_{0};u;x_{0})
.
Indeed, there is a
j\in\{1,\dots,k-2\}
such that

\varepsilon_{i_{j}}\varepsilon_{i_{j+1}}\varepsilon_{i_{j+2}}=x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}
and then at least one of factors
I(\varepsilon_{i_{j}};\varepsilon_{i_{j}+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{j+1}-1};\varepsilon_{i_{j+1}})
and
I(\varepsilon_{i_{j+1}};\varepsilon_{i_{j+1}+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{j+2}-1};\varepsilon_{i_{j+2}})
vanishes since
w\in\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
implies that
x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}
is not a strict subword of
w
and thus at least one of the quotient words is not empty (cf Notation
3.27
).
One similarly shows that, if
x_{1}x_{1}
is a strict subword of
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}
, then we obtain a vanishing factor
I(x_{1};u;x_{1})
.
Hence any subword
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}\notin\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
contributes trivially and the claim follows.
∎

In particular, by Lemma
4.3
the Goncharov coproduct restricts to

\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}\colon F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\otimes F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}.

Definition 4.4
.

For each
r\in\mathbb{N}
, define the map
\partial_{2r+1}:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\to\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle_{2r+1}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
by

\partial_{2r+1}(w)=\sum_{j=0}^{N-2r-1}I(\varepsilon_{j};\varepsilon_{j+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{j+2r+1};\varepsilon_{j+2r+2})\otimes\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}\cdots\varepsilon_{N}

for a word
w=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{N}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
and
\varepsilon_{0}=x_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{N+1}=x_{0}
.

Remark 4.5
.

Let
r\in\mathbb{N}
. By Proposition
3.30
, the linear map
\partial_{2r+1}
fits into the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{2r+1}}
\scriptstyle{D_{2r+1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle_{2r+1}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2r+1}\otimes\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{2r+1}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle.}

The maps
\partial_{2r+1}
introduced in
Definition
4.4
reduce the level.

Lemma 4.6
.

For all
r,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\partial_{2r+1}(F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3})\subseteq\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle_{2r+1}\otimes F_{\ell-1}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}.

Proof.

Let
w=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
. By Definition
4.4
, each summand in
\partial_{2r+1}(w)
corresponds to a strict subword
u=\varepsilon_{j+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{j+2r+1}
with
0\leq j\leq n-2r-1
of
w
.
If the enlarged word
\hat{u}=\varepsilon_{j}\cdots\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}
does not contain
x_{0}x_{0}
as a strict subword then it has to be
(x_{0}x_{1})^{r+1}x_{0}
or

x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{r+1}
since it cannot contain
x_{1}x_{1}
and we always have
\varepsilon_{0}\varepsilon_{1}=x_{1}x_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{n}\varepsilon_{n+1}=x_{1}x_{0}
by definition.
In either case, we have
\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}
and therefore the factor
I(\varepsilon_{j};u;\varepsilon_{j+2r+2})
vanishes.
We deduce that if the strict subword
u
contributes to
\partial_{2r+1}(w)
, then
\hat{u}
contains
x_{0}x_{0}
as a strict subword and we have
\varepsilon_{j}\neq\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}
. Hence
\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
implies that
\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in F_{\ell-1}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
.
∎

Definition 4.7
.

For all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we set

\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{F_{\ell-1}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}$}}{\raisebox{3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{F_{\ell-1}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}$}}{\raisebox{2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{F_{\ell-1}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}$}}{\raisebox{1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.70833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{F_{\ell-1}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}$}}}

and
\operatorname{gr}_{0}^{F}=F_{0}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
. For all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
we denote by
\pi_{\ell}^{F}\colon F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}

the natural projections.

By Lemma
4.6
, the restricted map
(\operatorname{id}\otimes\pi_{\ell-1}^{F})\circ\partial_{2r+1}\Big\rvert_{F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}}
induces a map

\partial_{2r+1}^{(\ell)}\colon\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle_{2r+1}\otimes\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}

for all
r,\ell\geq 1
.

Definition 4.8
.

Define the sub vector space

\mathcal{B}^{1}=\operatorname{gr}_{1}^{F}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}\oplus\,(\operatorname{gr}_{0}^{F}\mathcal{B}_{2,3})x_{0}\subset\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle.

Explicitly, we have

\mathcal{B}^{1}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\pi_{1}^{F}((x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{1}}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{2}})\mid r_{1},r_{2}\geq 0\}\oplus\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{(x_{0}x_{1})^{r}x_{0}\mid r\geq 0.\}

We have the following refinement of Lemma
4.6
.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
r,\ell\geq 1
. Then for each weight
N\geq 2r+1
, we have

\partial_{2r+1}^{(\ell)}\colon\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}^{1}\otimes\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N-2r-1}.

(31)

Proof.

Let
w=\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{N}\in\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N}
and
u=\varepsilon_{j+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{j+2r+1}
with
0\leq j\leq N-2r-1
, be a strict subword of
w
.
Assume the enlarged word
\hat{u}=\varepsilon_{j}\cdots\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}
contains
x_{0}x_{0}
at least two times as a strict subword. Then the summand in
\partial_{2r+1}(w)

correspoding to
u
is contained in
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle_{2r+1}\otimes F_{\ell-2}^{F}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
and thus vanishes under
\operatorname{id}\otimes\pi_{\ell-1}^{F}
.
On the other hand, if the choice of
u
contributes non-trivially to
\partial_{2r+1}(w)
then it must contain
x_{0}x_{0}
at least once as a strict subword
by Lemma
4.6
.
So we conclude, if
u
contributes non-trivially, then it contains
x_{0}x_{0}
exactly once as a strict subword.
The remaining cases for
u
determine the factors

1.

I\big(x_{1};(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{1}}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{2}};x_{0}\big)=(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{1}}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{2}}
,

2.

I\big(x_{0};(x_{1}x_{0})^{r_{1}}x_{1}x_{0}x_{0}(x_{1}x_{0})^{r_{2}};x_{1}\big)=-(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{2}}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{1}}
,

3.

I\big(x_{1};(x_{0}x_{1})^{r}x_{0};x_{0}\big)=(x_{0}x_{1})^{r}x_{0}
,

4.

I\big(x_{0};(x_{0}x_{1})^{r}x_{0};x_{1}\big)=-(x_{0}x_{1})^{r}x_{0}
.

By definition, all of these factors are mapped to elements of
\mathcal{B}^{1}
. Furthermore, we deduce from
\varepsilon_{j}\neq\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}
that
\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}\cdots\varepsilon_{N}\in\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N-2r-1}
and the claim follows.
∎

.
