Remark 4.11
.

For any word
w\in\mathcal{B}_{2,3}
there is always one
x_{0}
left to a letter
x_{1}
. Therefore, if in case 1. from the proof of Theorem
4.9

\hat{u}=\varepsilon_{j}\varepsilon_{j+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{j+2r+2}=x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{1}}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{r_{2}}x_{0}

is a strict subword of
w
or equivalently if
j>1
,
then we get also a case 2. contribution by

\hat{u}^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{j-1}\varepsilon_{j}\cdots\varepsilon_{j+2r+1}=x_{0}(x_{1}x_{0})^{r_{1}}x_{1}x_{0}x_{0}(x_{1}x_{0})^{r_{2}}x_{1}.

Conversely, each
\hat{u}^{\prime}
in case 2. determines a possibly enlarged strict subword
s
, as right to a letter
x_{1}
must always be a
x_{0}
.

The dotted and dashed lines in the above picture depict such a pair of contributing subwords
\hat{u}
and
\hat{u}^{\prime}
.

Because of the commutative diagram in Remark
4.5
, Lemma
4.6
and Theorem
4.9
imply the following for the derivations
D_{2r+1}
.

Proposition 4.12
.

The following holds.

1.

For all
\ell,r\in\mathbb{N}
we have

D_{2r+1}(F_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{2,3})\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{2r+1}\otimes F_{\ell-1}\mathcal{B}_{2,3}.

2.

For all
\ell,r\in\mathbb{N}
and each weight
N\geq 2r+1
we have derivations

D_{2r+1}^{(\ell)}\colon\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N}\rightarrow\pi_{2r+1}\big(\mathcal{B}^{1}\big)\otimes\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N-2r-1}.

4.2
The linear map
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}
is an isomorphism

Let
N,\ell,r\in\mathbb{N}
be positive integers throughout the rest of this subsection.

Definition 4.13
.

We define a linear map

\displaystyle\phi\colon\mathcal{B}^{1}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Q},

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\mapsto c_{\beta}=\begin{cases}c_{a,b}&\mbox{ if }\beta\in\operatorname{gr}_{1}^{F}\big(\mathcal{B}_{2,3}\big)\\
2\cdot(-1)^{n}&\mbox{ if }\beta\in\operatorname{gr}_{0}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})x_{0}.\end{cases}

where

c_{a,b}^{r}=2\cdot(-1)^{r}\left(\binom{2r}{2b+2}-(1-2^{-2r})\binom{2r}{2a+1}\right).

The behaviour of the numbers
c_{a,b}^{r}
will be discussed in detail in Subsection
4.3
.

Composing the map

\partial_{2r+1}^{(\ell)}\colon\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}^{1}\otimes\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N-2r-1}.

from Theorem
4.9
with
\phi
on the first factor yields a map

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{2r+1}\colon\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N-2r-1}

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\mapsto\phi\big(\partial_{2r+1}^{(\ell)}(w)\big).

Here, we identified
\mathbb{Q}\otimes\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N-2r-1}\simeq\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N-2r-1}
.

Definition 4.14
.

For
N\geq 2r+1
and
\ell\geq 1
we define the linear map

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}\colon\operatorname{gr}_{\ell}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\bigoplus_{3\leq 2r+1\leq N}\operatorname{gr}_{\ell-1}^{F}(\mathcal{B}_{2,3})_{N-2r-1}

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{3\leq 2r+1\leq N}\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{2r+1}(w).

Our goal is to show Theorem
4.27
, which says that
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}
is an isomorphism.
We begin by fixing a basis for the domain and codomain of
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}
, respectively.

Definition 4.15
.

Recall the map
\mathsf{b}\colon\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}^{*}\to\mathcal{X}^{*}
was given in Definition
4.2
. We set

\displaystyle B_{N}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{b}\big(\{u\in\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}^{*}\mid\operatorname{wt}(u)=N\text{ and }\deg_{3}(u)=\ell\}\big),

\displaystyle B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathsf{b}\big(\{u\in\{\mathsf{2},\mathsf{3}\}^{*}\mid\operatorname{wt}(u)<N-1,\,\,\deg_{3}(u)=\ell-1\}\big),&N\equiv\ell\bmod 2\\
\emptyset&N\not\equiv\ell\bmod 2\end{cases}.

Remark 4.16
.

It is easy to see that also
B_{N}^{(\ell)}
is empty, if
N\not\equiv\ell\mod 2
.

If
B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}\neq\emptyset
, then it is the disjoint union of lower weights, i. e.

B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}=\mathop{\dot{\bigcup}}_{n=0}^{N-3}B_{n}^{(\ell-1)}.

Lemma 4.17
.

The map

\displaystyle\psi\colon B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}

\displaystyle\rightarrow B_{N}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle\mathsf{b}(u)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathsf{b}\big(\mathsf{2}^{r-1}\mathsf{3}u\big)

where
2r=N-1-\operatorname{wt}(u)
is a bijection.

Proof.

If
B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}=\emptyset
, we also have
B_{N}^{(\ell)}=\emptyset
and vice versa by Remark
4.16
.
So we can assume without loss of generality that
B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}\neq\emptyset
. Let
\mathsf{b}(u)\in B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}
.
Indeed,
\mathsf{b}\big(\mathsf{2}^{r-1}\mathsf{3}u\big)\in B_{N}^{(\ell)}
by the choice of
r
.
Since
\ell\geq 1
, all words in
B_{N}^{(\ell)}
can be uniquely written as
\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{2}^{s-1}\mathsf{3}u^{\prime})
for some
s\in\mathbb{N}
and a word
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
with
\mathsf{b}(u^{\prime})\in B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}
.
The assignment
\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{2}^{s-1}\mathsf{3}u^{\prime})\mapsto\mathsf{b}(u^{\prime})
clearly gives an inverse map to
\psi
. Hence
\psi
is a bijection, since both sets are finite.
∎

Corollary 4.18
.

We have

\#B_{N}^{(\ell)}=\#B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}.

∎

Remark 4.19
.

The statement in Corollary
4.18
can also be proven directly by combinatorial means.
We again disregard the case where either set is empty (cf. Remark
4.16
).
Then we have
N=2m+3\ell
for some non-negative
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and observe that

\#B_{N}^{(\ell)}=\binom{m+\ell}{\ell}=\sum_{0\leq m^{\prime}\leq m}\binom{m^{\prime}+\ell-1}{\ell-1}=\#B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)},

where the second equality is the so-called hockey-stick identity of binomial coefficients.

Definition 4.20
.

We endow
B_{N}^{(\ell)}
with the lexicographic order
\leq_{\text{lex}}
with respect to the order
\mathsf{2}<\mathsf{3}
. With this we define an order on

B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}
by
\mathsf{b}(u)\leq\mathsf{b}(u^{\prime})
, iff

\psi(\mathsf{b}(u))\leq_{\text{lex}}\psi(\mathsf{b}(u)^{\prime})
.

In other words, in Definition
4.20
we require that the bijection
\psi
is an order preserving map. Observe we get

\mathsf{b}(u)\leq\mathsf{b}(u^{\prime})
if and only if
\operatorname{wt}(u)<\operatorname{wt}(u^{\prime})
or if
\operatorname{wt}(u)=\operatorname{wt}(u^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{b}(u)\leq_{\text{lex}}\mathsf{b}(u^{\prime})

The same order is used in
[
10
]
.

Remark 4.21
.

Brown
[
9
]
uses a similar lexicographic order on
B_{N}^{(\ell)}
, but a different order on
B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}
.
The order on
B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}
can be recovered from the lexicographic order on
B_{N}^{(\ell)}
via a variation of the map
\psi\colon B_{N}^{(\ell)}\to B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}

from Lemma
4.17
given by

u\mapsto u\mathsf{3}\mathsf{2}^{s-1}
.
This difference in Brown’s setup is due to the inverted MZV-notation.

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
w\in B_{N}^{(\ell)}
,
r\geq 1
. If there is a decomposition
w=\beta v
with
\beta\in B_{2r+1}^{(1)}
, then

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{2r+1}(w)=c_{\beta}\,v+(\text{terms with coefficients in $2\mathbb{Z}$}).

Otherwise,
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{2r+1}(w)
is given by a sum of terms with coefficients in
2\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Recall the four types of strict subwords
w
that contribute non-trivially to
\partial_{2r+1}^{(\ell)}(w)
from the proof of Theorem
4.9
.

Unless
w=\beta v
, the first two cases come in pairs
\beta,\beta^{\prime}
by Remark
4.11
. They contribute with
c_{\beta}=c_{a,b}
and
c_{\beta^{\prime}}=-c_{b,a}
for some non-negative
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
. By Lemma
4.32
2. we have
c_{\beta}+c_{\beta^{\prime}}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
.

The last two cases contribute with the even coefficients
\pm 2
.
∎

We deduce for the linear maps
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}
from
Definition
4.14

the following from Proposition
4.22
.

Proposition 4.23
.

For
w\in B_{N}^{(\ell)}
we have

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}(w)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w=\beta v,\\
\beta\in B_{2r+1}^{(1)},r\geq 1\end{subarray}}c_{\beta}\,v+(\text{terms with coefficients in }2\mathbb{Z}).

∎

Definition 4.24
.

Let
N,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
with
N=2m+3\ell
for some non-negative
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. We define

M_{N}^{(\ell)}=\big(m_{w,w^{\prime}}\big)_{w\in B_{N}^{(\ell)},\,w^{\prime}\in B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}}

to be the representing matrix of
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}
with respect to the bases
B_{N}^{(\ell)}
and
B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}
from Definition
4.20
.

Precisely,
m_{w,w^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Q}
are for
w\in B_{N}^{(\ell)}
defined by the equations

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}(w)=\sum_{w^{\prime}\in B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}}m_{w,w^{\prime}}\,w^{\prime}.

We now deduce the following from Corollary
4.18
.

Corollary 4.25
.

The matrix
M_{N}^{(\ell)}
is quadratic. ∎

Recall that our goal is to show that the level reducing map
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}
is an isomorphism.
By Remark
4.16
, it suffices to study the non-trivial cases where
N=2m+3\ell
for some non-negative
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
The key idea of the proof is showing that the transformation matrices
M_{N}^{(\ell)}
are invertible via the following lemma. Therefore, recall the
p
-adic valuation
\nu_{p}
of a rational number also given in Definition
4.31
.

Lemma 4.26
.

Let
p
be a prime number and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
A=(a_{ij})
be an
n\times n
matrix with entries in
\mathbb{Q}
such that

(i)

\nu_{p}(a_{ij})\geq 1
for all
i>j
and

(ii)

\nu_{p}(a_{jj})=\min\{\nu_{p}(a_{ij})\mid i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\}\leq 0
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Then
A
is invertible.

Proof.

To show that
\det(A)\neq 0
it suffices to show that
\det(A^{\prime})\neq 0
where the matrix
A^{\prime}=(a_{ij}^{\prime})
arises from
A
by multiplying the
j
-th column with
p^{-\nu_{p}(a_{jj})}
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Since
\nu_{p}(a_{jj})\leq 0
, condition (i) still holds for
A^{\prime}
. Condition (ii) implies that
\nu_{p}(a_{ij}^{\prime})\geq 0
for all entries of
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
By construction of
A^{\prime}
, we have
\nu_{p}(a_{jj}^{\prime})=0
. So in particular, the entries on the diagonal are not zero.
It follows that
A^{\prime}
modulo
p
is an upper triangular matrix with non-zero entries on the diagonal and is thus invertible.
∎

Theorem 4.27

(Brown
[
9
]
)
.

The map
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}
is an isomorphism of vector spaces.

Proof.

We show that the matrix
M_{N}^{(\ell)}
of the operator
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}
, given in Definition
4.24
, is invertible, since it satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
4.26
for
p=2
.

Let
v\in B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}
. Recall the bijection
\psi\colon B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}\to B_{N}^{(\ell)}
from Lemma
4.17
which is order-preserving by Definition
4.20
. Let
2r=N-1-\operatorname{wt}(v)
and let
w\in B_{N}^{(\ell)}
with
w=\beta v
for some
\beta\in B_{2r+1}^{(1)}
.
If
w\neq\psi(v)
, then
\beta
does not end in
x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}
, hence
w>\psi(v)
.
We deduce from Proposition
4.23
that the entries of
M_{N}^{(\ell)}
that are not in
2\mathbb{Z}
are either on or above the main diagonal due to the orders on
B_{N}^{(\ell)}
and
B_{<N}^{(\ell-1)}
. This implies condition (i) from Lemma
4.26
.

The entries on the main diagonal of
M_{N}^{(\ell)}
are given by the coefficients of
v
in
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(\ell)}_{<N}(\psi(v))
with
r
as above. By Lemma
4.32
3. and Proposition
4.23
, these entries have a non-positive
2
-adic valuation and realize the minimum of this valuation within its column. Hence
M_{N}^{(\ell)}
satisfies condition (ii) from Lemma
4.26
.
∎

We illustrate the content of this section in two examples.

Example 4.28
.

In our first example, we compute the matrix
M_{9}^{(1)}
corresponding to the map
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(1)}_{<9}
.

We have

\displaystyle B_{9}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,2),\mathsf{b}(2,3,2,2),\mathsf{b}(2,2,3,2),\mathsf{b}(2,2,2,3)\}

and

\displaystyle B_{<9}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{b}(2,2,2),\mathsf{b}(2,2),\mathsf{b}(2),\mathbf{1}\}.

In order to compute, e. g.,
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(1)}_{<9}(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,2))
we first observe that

\displaystyle\partial_{3}^{(1)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,2)\big)

\displaystyle=I(x_{1};x_{0}x_{0}x_{1};x_{0})\otimes\mathsf{b}(2,2,2)+I(x_{0};x_{0}x_{1}x_{0};x_{1})\otimes\mathsf{b}(2,2,2)

\displaystyle=(x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}-x_{0}x_{1}x_{0})\otimes\mathsf{b}(2,2,2).

These terms can be depicted, respectively, as follows:
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{1}
x_{1}
x_{1}
x_{1}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{1}
x_{1}
x_{1}
x_{1}
x_{1}
x_{0}

Since
\phi(x_{0}x_{0}x_{1})=2
and
\phi(x_{0}x_{1}x_{0})=-1
(cf. Definition
4.13
) we obtain that

(\phi\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\partial_{3}^{(1)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,2)\big)=3\otimes\mathsf{b}(2,2,2)

which we identify with
3\,\mathsf{b}(2,2,2)
. Similarly, one computes that

\displaystyle\partial_{5}^{(1)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,2)\big)=

\displaystyle(x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}x_{0}x_{1}-2\,(x_{0}x_{1})^{2}x_{0})\otimes\mathsf{b}(2,2),

\displaystyle\partial_{7}^{(1)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,2)\big)=

\displaystyle(x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{2}+2\,(x_{0}x_{1})^{3}x_{0})\otimes\mathsf{b}(2),

\displaystyle\partial_{9}^{(1)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,2)\big)=

\displaystyle x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{3}\otimes\mathbf{1}.

Thus we obtain

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(1)}_{<9}(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,2))=3\,\mathsf{b}(2,2,2)-\frac{15}{2}\,\mathsf{b}(2,2)+\frac{189}{16}\,\mathsf{b}(2)-\frac{223}{16}.

Similar computations for the remaining words in
B_{9}^{(1)}
give

M_{9}^{(1)}=\begin{pmatrix}3&-\frac{15}{2}&\frac{189}{16}&-\frac{223}{16}\\[4.30554pt]
0&-\frac{15}{2}&\frac{299}{8}&-\frac{889}{16}\\[4.30554pt]
0&2&-\frac{291}{16}&\frac{455}{16}\\[4.30554pt]
-2&12&-30&\frac{641}{16}\end{pmatrix}.

Example 4.29
.

In our second example, we compute the matrix
M_{10}^{(2)}
. We have

\displaystyle B_{10}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{b}(3,3,2,2),\mathsf{b}(3,2,3,2),\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,3),\mathsf{b}(2,3,3,2),\mathsf{b}(2,3,2,3),\mathsf{b}(2,2,3,3)\}

and

\displaystyle B_{<10}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{b}(3,2,2),\mathsf{b}(2,3,2),\mathsf{b}(2,2,3),\mathsf{b}(3,2),\mathsf{b}(2,3),\mathsf{b}(3)\}.

Similar to the previous example, one computes that

\displaystyle\partial_{3}^{(2)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,3)\big)

\displaystyle=(x_{0}x_{1}x_{0}-x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}+x_{0}x_{0}x_{1})\otimes\mathsf{b}(3,2,2)

\displaystyle+(x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}-x_{0}x_{1}x_{0})\otimes\mathsf{b}(2,2,3),

\displaystyle\partial_{5}^{(2)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,3)\big)

\displaystyle=(x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}x_{0}x_{1}-(x_{0}x_{1})^{2}x_{0})\otimes\mathsf{b}(2,3)

\displaystyle+((x_{0}x_{1})^{2}x_{0}-x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}x_{0}x_{1}+x_{0}x_{1}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1})\otimes\mathsf{b}(3,2),

\displaystyle\partial_{7}^{(2)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,3)\big)

\displaystyle=(x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{2}-x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{2}+(x_{0}x_{1})^{2}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1})\otimes\mathsf{b}(3),

\displaystyle\partial_{9}^{(2)}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,3)\big)

\displaystyle=0.

By applying
\phi
we obtain

\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 5.30833pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 6.67831pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}{\mathop{}\kern 7.5657pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\partial}}^{\mathmakebox[0pt][l]{\mathmakebox[r]{\phi}}}}\partial^{(2)}_{<10}\big(\mathsf{b}(3,2,2,3)\big)=-2\,\mathsf{b}(3,2,2)+3\,\mathsf{b}(2,2,3)+12\,\mathsf{b}(3,2)-\frac{15}{2}\,\mathsf{b}(2,3)-\frac{291}{16}\,\mathsf{b}(3).

Similar computations for the remaining elements of
B_{10}^{(2)}
yield

M_{10}^{(2)}=\begin{pmatrix}3&0&0&-12&0&28\\[4.30554pt]
0&3&0&-\frac{11}{2}&0&0\\[4.30554pt]
-2&0&3&12&-\frac{15}{2}&-\frac{291}{16}\\[4.30554pt]
0&0&0&\frac{9}{2}&-10&0\\[4.30554pt]
0&-2&0&0&\frac{9}{2}&\frac{75}{8}\\[4.30554pt]
0&0&-2&0&12&-\frac{291}{16}\\[4.30554pt]
\end{pmatrix}.

We conclude the examples with the observations that both
M_{9}^{(1)}
and
M_{10}^{(2)}
are invertible since

\displaystyle\det(M_{9}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=\frac{4865}{512}

\displaystyle\det(M_{10}^{(2)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{435419}{64}

and that all entries below the main diagonal are in
2\mathbb{Z}
, respectively.

4.3
Some properties of the numbers
c_{a,b}^{r}

In this subsection we present some of the arithmetic properties of the numbers
c_{a,b}^{r}
introduced in Definition
4.13
. Later in Section
6.2

these numbers occur in Zagier’s Theorem and will play a crucial role in the main result of these notes.

Definition 4.30
.

For integers
a,b,r\geq 0
we set

\displaystyle A_{a,b}^{r}

\displaystyle=(1-2^{-2r})\binom{2r}{2a+1},

\displaystyle B_{a,b}^{r}

\displaystyle=\binom{2r}{2b+2}.

Then, we get immediately

c_{a,b}^{r}=2\cdot(-1)^{r}(B_{a,b}^{r}-A_{a,b}^{r}).

Definition 4.31
.

Let
p
be a prime number and
q\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
non-zero. The
p
-adic valuation

\nu_{p}(q)
of
q
is the integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
q=p^{n}\frac{a}{b}
with
a,b
relatively prime to
p
. We further set
\nu_{p}(0)=\infty
.

It is not hard to verify that
p
-adic valuations satisfy the following basic properties for
q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}

\displaystyle\nu_{p}(q_{1}\cdot q_{2})

\displaystyle=\nu_{p}(q_{1})+\nu_{p}(q_{2})

(32)

\displaystyle\nu_{p}(q_{1}+q_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\min\{\nu_{p}(q_{1}),\nu_{p}(q_{2})\}

(33)

with equality in (
33
) if
\nu_{p}(q_{1})\neq\nu_{p}(q_{2})
.

Lemma 4.32
.

For all integers
a,b\geq 0
we define

c_{a,b}=c_{a,b}^{a+b+1}.

These numbers satisfy

1.

c_{a,b}\in\mathbb{Z}[\frac{1}{2}]
,

2.

c_{a,b}-c_{b,a}\in 2\mathbb{Z}
and

3.

\nu_{2}(c_{a+b,0})=\nu_{2}(c_{0,a+b})\leq\nu_{2}(c_{a,b})\leq 0
.

Proof.

Write
n=a+b+1
.
The first claim follows immediately from the definition

c_{a,b}=2\cdot(-1)^{n}\left(\binom{2n}{2b+2}-(1-2^{-2n})\binom{2n}{2a+1}\right).

The second claim follows from (
34
), i. e.
A_{a,b}^{n}-A_{b,a}^{n}=0
hence

c_{a,b}-c_{b,a}=2\cdot(-1)^{n}(B_{a,b}^{n}-B_{b,a}^{n})\in 2\mathbb{Z}.

It remains to prove the third claim. We first show that
\nu_{2}((2n)!)<2n
. By (
32
) we have

\nu_{2}((2n)!)=\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\nu_{2}(i).

We rearrange this sum and obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\nu_{2}(i)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor\log_{2}(2n)\rfloor}\#\big\{i\in\{1,\dots,2n\}\mid\text{$i$ is divisible by $2^{k}$}\big\}

\displaystyle=n+\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor+\left\lfloor\frac{n}{4}\right\rfloor+\dots+1<2n.

It follows that

\nu_{2}\left(2^{-2n}\cdot\binom{2n}{2a+1}\right)<0.

On the other hand, we clearly have
\nu_{2}\big(\binom{2n}{2b+2}-\binom{2n}{2a+1}\big)\geq 0
.
In particular, (
33
) becomes an equality for
q_{1}=2^{-2n}\cdot\binom{2n}{2a+1}
and
q_{2}=\binom{2n}{2b+2}-\binom{2n}{2a+1}
, hence

\displaystyle\nu_{2}(c_{a,b})

\displaystyle=1+\nu_{2}\left(2^{-2n}\cdot\binom{2n}{2a+1}+\binom{2n}{2b+2}-\binom{2n}{2a+1}\right)

\displaystyle=1+\nu_{2}\left(2^{-2n}\cdot\binom{2n}{2a+1}\right)\leq 0.

This proves the last inequality in the third claim.
By writing
\binom{2n}{2a+1}=\frac{2n}{2a+1}\binom{2n-1}{2a}
we further obtain from (
32
) that

\nu_{2}(c_{a,b})=1+\nu_{2}\left(2^{-2n}\cdot\frac{2n}{2a+1}\binom{2n-1}{2a}\right)=2-2n+\nu_{2}(n)+\nu_{2}\left(\binom{2n-1}{2a}\right).

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
fixed, this is minimal for
a=0
and
a=n-1
. The latter case is equivalent to
b=0
.
∎

Later in Subsection
6.2
we need the following lemma.

Lemma 4.33
.

For
a,b\geq 0
and
1\leq r\leq a+b+1
we have

c_{a,b}^{r}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}c_{\beta,\alpha}^{r}+2\cdot(-1)^{r}\big(\mathbb{I}(a\geq r)-\mathbb{I}(b\geq r)\big).

Here,
\mathbb{I}
denotes the indicator function, i.e.
\mathbb{I}(A)=1
if
A
is a true statement and
\mathbb{I}(A)=0
else.

Proof.

Using the quantities
A_{a,b}^{r}
and
B_{a,b}^{r}
, it suffices to show that

B_{a,b}^{r}-A_{a,b}^{r}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}(B_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-A_{\alpha,\beta}^{r})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}(B_{\beta,\alpha}^{r}-A_{\beta,\alpha}^{r})+(\mathbb{I}(a\geq r)-\mathbb{I}(b\geq r))

for
a,b\geq 0
and
1\leq r\leq a+b+1
.

We first show for all
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
and
r=\alpha+\beta+1
that

\displaystyle A_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}

\displaystyle=A_{\beta,\alpha}^{r}

(34)

\displaystyle B_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}

\displaystyle=B_{\beta+1,\alpha-1}^{r}.

(35)

Recall that we have for
0\leq k\leq n

\binom{n}{k}=\binom{n}{n-k}.

Identity (
34
) follows immediately for
n=2r
and
k=2\alpha+1
. Identity (
35
) follows for
n=2r
and
k=2\beta+2
from

2(\alpha+\beta+1)-(2\beta+2)=2\alpha=2(\alpha-1)+2.

It suffices to show that

\displaystyle A_{a,b}^{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}A_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}A_{\beta,\alpha}^{r}

(36)

\displaystyle B_{a,b}^{r}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}B_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}B_{\beta,\alpha}^{r}+\mathbb{I}(a\geq r)-\mathbb{I}(b\geq r).

(37)

We first observe that
a\geq r
is equivalent to

1.

A_{a,\beta}^{r}
vanishes for all
\beta\geq 0
,

2.

the summand for
\alpha=a
does not appear in the first sum since
\alpha+\beta+1=r
would imply
\beta<0
and

3.

the summand for
\beta=0
appears in the second sum since then
\alpha=r-1<a
.

We deduce (
36
) from the first two observations since

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}A_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}A_{\beta,\alpha}^{r}=A_{a,b}^{r}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}(A_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-A_{\beta,\alpha}^{r})\overset{\eqref{eq:final_lemma_claim_A}}{=}A_{a,b}^{r}.

We similarly observe for identity (
37
) that
b\geq r
is equivalent to

1.

B_{\alpha,b}^{r}
vanishes for all
\alpha\geq 0
,

2.

the summand for
\beta=b
does not appear in the first sum since
\alpha+\beta+1=r
would imply
\alpha<0
and

3.

the summand for
\alpha=0
appears in the first sum since then
\beta=r-1<b
.

So we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}B_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}B_{\beta,\alpha}^{r}+\mathbb{I}(a\geq r)-\mathbb{I}(b\geq r)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:final_lemma_claim_B}}{=}B_{a,b}^{r}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta<b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}B_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0\leq\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}B_{\alpha+1,\beta-1}^{r}+\mathbb{I}(a\geq r)-\mathbb{I}(b\geq r)

\displaystyle=B_{a,b}^{r}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta<b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}B_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}+\mathbb{I}(b\geq r)-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha<a\\
0<\beta\leq b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}B_{\alpha+1,\beta-1}^{r}-\mathbb{I}(a\geq r)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{I}(a\geq r)-\mathbb{I}(b\geq r)

\displaystyle=B_{a,b}^{r}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\alpha\leq a\\
0\leq\beta<b\\
\alpha+\beta+1=r\end{subarray}}\Big(B_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}-B_{\alpha,\beta}^{r}\Big)

\displaystyle=B_{a,b}^{r},

where the second equality follows from the third observations above for
a\geq r
and
b\geq r
, respectively, and the third equality follows from a shift of indices.
∎

5
Formal multiple zeta values

5.1
Multiple zeta values and their general structure

We provide a short basic introduction in the theory of multiple zeta values, which will serve as the motivation for formal multiple zeta values. For a detailed exposition we refer to
[
6
]
,
[
10
]
,
[
31
]
.

Definition 5.1
.

To integers
k_{1}\geq 2,\ k_{2},\ldots,k_{d}\geq 1
, associate the
multiple zeta value

\zeta(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})=\sum_{n_{1}>\dots>n_{d}>0}\frac{1}{n_{1}^{k_{1}}\dots n_{d}^{k_{d}}}\in\mathbb{R}.

Denote the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space spanned by all multiple zeta values by

\Z=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\zeta(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})\mid d\geq 0,\ k_{1}\geq 2,\ k_{2},\ldots,k_{d}\geq 1\},

where
\zeta(\emptyset)=1
.
For an index
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, define the
weight
and
depth
by

\displaystyle\operatorname{wt}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})=k_{1}+\dots+k_{d},\qquad\operatorname{dep}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})=d.

For simplicity, we will also refer to these numbers as the weight and depth of
\zeta(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})
.

Numerical experiments have led to the following dimension conjectures for
\Z
.

Conjecture 5.2
.

(
[
43
, p. 509]
)

1) The vector space
\Z
is graded with respect to the weight, i.e.,

\Z=\bigoplus\limits_{w\geq 0}\Z_{w},

where
\Z_{w}
is spanned by all multiple zeta values of weight
w
.

2) The dimensions of the homogeneous subspaces
\Z_{w}
are given by

H_{\Z}(x)=\sum_{w\geq 0}\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Z_{w})x^{w}=\frac{1}{1-x^{2}-x^{3}}.

This conjecture implies that the numbers
d_{w}=\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}(\Z_{w})
should satisfy the recursion

\displaystyle d_{w}=d_{w-2}+d_{w-3}

with initial values
d_{1}=0
and
d_{2}=d_{3}=1
.

It is well-known that
\Z
is not graded with respect to the depth, e.g., there is Euler’s relation

\displaystyle\zeta(2,1)=\zeta(3).

(38)

In these lectures we just want to mention the Broadhurst-Kreimer conjecture (
[
8
, (7)]
), which is a refinement of Zagier’s dimension conjecture
that in addition relies also on the depth filtration.

There exists also a suggestion for an explicit basis for
\Z
. We set

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Q}}\{\zeta(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})\mid k_{i}\in\{2,3\}\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,d\}\subset\Z.

Conjecture 5.3
.

(
[
29
, Conjecture C]
) A basis for
\Z
is given by the Hoffman elements
\zeta(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})
,
k_{i}\in\{2,3\}
. In particular, we have

\mathcal{H}=\Z.

This conjecture would imply Zagier’s dimension conjecture
5.2
.

It is quite obvious, that there exists more structure on the space
\Z
.

Proposition 5.4
.

The space
\Z
equipped with the usual multiplication of real numbers is an algebra. ∎

There are two ways of expressing the product of multiple zeta values, called the
stuffle
and the
shuffle product
. The stuffle product comes from the combinatorics of multiplying infinite nested sums. E.g., for
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 2
, there is the simple calculation

\displaystyle\zeta(k_{1})\zeta(k_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{m>0}\frac{1}{m^{k_{1}}}\right)\left(\sum_{n>0}\frac{1}{n^{k_{2}}}\right)=\left(\sum_{m>n>0}+\sum_{n>m>0}+\sum_{m=n>0}\right)\frac{1}{m^{k_{1}}n^{k_{2}}}

\displaystyle=\zeta(k_{1},k_{2})+\zeta(k_{2},k_{1})+\zeta(k_{1}+k_{2}).

The shuffle product is obtained from expressing multiple zeta values as iterated integrals (
[
10
, Theorem 1.108.]
). E.g., in depth
2
the shuffle product reads for
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 2

\zeta(k_{1})\zeta(k_{2})=\sum_{j=2}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-1}\left(\binom{j-1}{k_{1}-1}+\binom{j-1}{k_{2}-1}\right)\zeta(j,k_{1}+k_{2}-j).

5.2
Extended double shuffle relations

To describe these two product expressions of multiple zeta values in general, we will use Hoffman’s quasi-shuffle Hopf algebras (Subsection
2.2
). The comparison of these two product formulas lead us then to the (extended) double shuffle relations.

The shuffle product of the multiple zeta values can be described in terms of the previously studied shuffle Hopf algebra
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})
.

Denote by
\mathfrak{h}^{0}
the subspace of
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
generated by
\mathbf{1}
and all words starting in
x_{0}
and ending in
x_{1}
, so

\mathfrak{h}^{0}=\mathbb{Q}\mathbf{1}+x_{0}\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle x_{1}.

Theorem 5.5
.

The map

\displaystyle\zeta:(\mathfrak{h}^{0},\shuffle)

\displaystyle\to(\Z,\cdot),

\displaystyle x_{0}^{k_{1}-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}-1}x_{1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\zeta(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})

is a surjective algebra morphism compatible with notions of weight and depth for words and indices.
∎

Recall that the
weight
of a word
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
is the number of its letters, and by the
depth
of a word
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
we mean the number of the letter
x_{1}
in
w
.

To describe the stuffle product of multiple zeta values, we introduce a new alphabet.

Definition 5.6
.

Consider the infinite alphabet
\mathcal{Y}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\ldots\}
. For a word in
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle
, define the
weight
and
depth
by

\displaystyle\operatorname{wt}(y_{k_{1}}\cdots y_{k_{d}})=k_{1}+\dots+k_{d},\qquad\operatorname{dep}(y_{k_{1}}\cdots y_{k_{d}})=d.

Let the
stuffle product

\ast
on
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle
be the quasi-shuffle product corresponding to

y_{i}\diamond y_{j}=y_{i+j}\qquad\text{ for }i,j\geq 1.

From Theorem
2.19
we obtain the following.

Proposition 5.7
.

The tuple
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle,\ast,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})
is a weight-graded commutative Hopf algebra. The complete dual Hopf algebra with respect to the pairing in (
5
) is given by
(R\langle\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\ast})
, where the coproduct
\Delta_{\ast}
is defined on the generators by

\Delta_{\ast}(y_{i})=y_{i}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes y_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}y_{j}\otimes y_{i-j},\quad i=1,2,\ldots.

Denote by
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle^{0}
the subspace of
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle
generated by all words, which do not start in
y_{1}
.

Theorem 5.8
.

The map

\displaystyle\zeta:(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle^{0},\ast)

\displaystyle\to(\Z,\cdot),

\displaystyle y_{k_{1}}\cdots y_{k_{d}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\zeta(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})

is a surjective algebra morphism compatible with the notions of weight and depth for words and indices.
∎

Comparing the shuffle and stuffle product formulas for multiple zeta values (Theorem
5.5
,
5.8
) gives the
(finite) double shuffle relations
among multiple zeta values. Euler’s relation given in (
38
) is not covered by the finite double shuffle relations, since there is no product decomposition in weight
3
. To get these kind of relations we will introduce regularizations.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
T
be a commutative variable and extend the stuffle product
\ast
by
\mathbb{Q}[T]
-linearity to
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle^{0}[T]
. We have an algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{reg}_{\ast}:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle^{0}[T]\to\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle,

\displaystyle wT^{n}\mapsto w\ast y_{1}^{\ast n}.

∎

For any
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle
, set

\displaystyle\zeta_{\ast}^{T}(w)=\zeta(\operatorname{reg}_{\ast}^{-1}(w))\in\Z[T],

where we extend also the map
\zeta:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle^{0}\to\Z
by
\mathbb{Q}[T]
-linearity to
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle^{0}[T]
. We call

\displaystyle\zeta_{\ast}(w)=\zeta_{\ast}^{T=0}(w)=\zeta(\operatorname{reg}_{\ast}^{-1}(w)|_{T=0})\in\Z

the
stuffle-regularized multiple zeta values
.
An immediate consequence of Proposition
5.9
is the following.

Theorem 5.10
.

The map
\zeta_{\ast}:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle\to\Z
given by
w\mapsto\zeta_{\ast}(w)
is the unique map satisfying

(i)

\zeta_{\ast}(w)=\zeta(w)
for all
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle^{0}
,

(ii)

\zeta_{\ast}(y_{1})=0
,

(iii)

\zeta_{\ast}(u)\zeta_{\ast}(v)=\zeta_{\ast}(u\ast v)
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle
.

Similarly, there also exists a regularization with respect to the shuffle product for multiple zeta values.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
T,U
be a commutative variables and extend the shuffle product
\shuffle
by
\mathbb{Q}[T,U]
-linearity to
\mathfrak{h}^{0}[T,U]
. There is an algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{reg}_{\shuffle}:\mathfrak{h}^{0}[T,U]

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,

\displaystyle wT^{n}U^{m}

\displaystyle\mapsto w\shuffle x_{1}^{\shuffle n}\shuffle x_{0}^{\shuffle m}.

∎

For any
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
, set

\displaystyle\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T,U}(w)=\zeta(\operatorname{reg}_{\shuffle}^{-1}(w))\in\Z[T,U],

where the map
\zeta:\mathfrak{h}^{0}\to\Z
needs to be extended by
\mathbb{Q}[T,U]
-linearity to
\mathfrak{h}^{0}[T,U]
. We set

\displaystyle\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(w)=\zeta(\operatorname{reg}_{\shuffle}^{-1}(w)|_{U=0})\in\Z[T],

and, moreover, call

\displaystyle\zeta_{\shuffle}(w)=\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T=U=0}(w)=\zeta(\operatorname{reg}_{\ast}^{-1}(w)|_{T=U=0})\in\Z

the
shuffle-regularized multiple zeta values
.
One derives the following from Proposition
5.11
.

Theorem 5.12
.

The map
\zeta_{\shuffle}:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\to\Z,\ w\mapsto\zeta_{\shuffle}(w)
is the unique map satisfying

(i)

\zeta_{\shuffle}(w)=\zeta(w)
for all
w\in\mathfrak{h}^{0}
,

(ii)

\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{1})=\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{0})=0
,

(iii)

\zeta_{\shuffle}(u)\zeta_{\shuffle}(v)=\zeta_{\shuffle}(u\shuffle v)
for all
u,v\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
.

We want to relate these two regularizations of multiple zeta values. Define the
\Z
-linear map
\rho:\Z[T]\to\Z[T]
by

\displaystyle\rho\left(\frac{T^{m}}{m!}\right)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\gamma_{i}\frac{T^{m-i}}{(m-i)!},\qquad m=0,1,2,\ldots,

(39)

where the coefficients
\gamma_{i}\in\Z
are defined by
\sum\limits_{i\geq 0}\gamma_{i}u^{i}=\exp\left(\sum\limits_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n}\zeta(n)u^{n}\right)
.

Theorem 5.13
.

(
[
31
, Theorem 1]
)
For all
k_{1},\ldots,k_{d}\geq 1
, one has

\rho\big(\zeta_{\ast}^{T}(y_{k_{1}}\cdots y_{k_{d}})\big)=\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(x_{0}^{k_{1}-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}-1}x_{1}).

∎

Combining the stuffle product formula for the stuffle-regularized multiple zeta values and the shuffle product formula for the shuffle-regularized multiple zeta values together with Theorem
5.13
gives the
extended double shuffle relations
among multiple zeta values.

Conjecture 5.14
.

(
[
31
, Conjecture 1]
) All algebraic relations in the algebra
\Z
of multiple zeta values are a consequence of the extended double shuffle relations.

In particular, Conjecture
5.14
would imply that the algebra
\Z
is graded by weight, since the stuffle and the shuffle product are both homogeneous for the weight.

Example 5.15
.

We calculate

\displaystyle\zeta_{\ast}(y_{1})\zeta_{\ast}(y_{2})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{\ast}(y_{1}y_{2})+\zeta_{\ast}(y_{2}y_{1})+\zeta_{\ast}(y_{3}),

\displaystyle\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{1})\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{1}x_{0}x_{1})+2\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{0}x_{1}x_{1})

By Theorem
5.13
, we have
\zeta_{\ast}(y_{1}y_{2})=\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{1}x_{0}x_{1})
, and, moreover,
\zeta_{\ast}(y_{1})=\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{1})=0
. Thus, the above equations reduce to

\displaystyle\zeta_{\ast}(y_{2}y_{1})+\zeta_{\ast}(y_{3})=2\zeta_{\shuffle}(x_{0}x_{1}x_{1}).

By Theorem
5.10
,
5.12
, this is equivalent to

\displaystyle\zeta(3)=\zeta(2,1).

So we recover Euler’s relations from the extended double shuffle relations.

Remark 5.16
.

There are various kinds of relations obtained for multiple zeta values in the literature, and several of them are expected to give all algebraic relations in
\Z
. A graphical overview is given by H. Bachmann in
[
6
, Section 3.3]
.

5.3
Formal multiple zeta values and some properties

We have a canonical embedding

\displaystyle\iota:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,

\displaystyle y_{k_{1}}\cdots y_{k_{d}}

\displaystyle\mapsto x_{0}^{k_{1}-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}-1}x_{1},

which allows to transfer the stuffle product
\ast
on
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle
(Definition
5.6
) to the algebra
\mathfrak{h}^{1}=\mathbb{Q}\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle x_{1}
.
So motivated by Conjecture
5.14
, we can reformulate the extended double shuffle relations purely algebraically in
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
.

Definition 5.17
.

The algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
of
formal multiple zeta values
is given by

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\displaystyle{\operatorname{R}_{\operatorname{EDS}}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.41666pt}{$\textstyle{\operatorname{R}_{\operatorname{EDS}}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.39166pt}{$\scriptstyle{\operatorname{R}_{\operatorname{EDS}}}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-1.6994pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\operatorname{R}_{\operatorname{EDS}}}$}}},

where
\operatorname{R}_{\operatorname{EDS}}
is the ideal generated by the extended double shuffle relations. Following
[
31
]
,
\operatorname{R}_{\operatorname{EDS}}
is for example generated by
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
and

u\shuffle v-\iota\Big(\iota^{-1}(u)\ast\iota^{-1}(v)\Big),\qquad u\in\mathfrak{h}^{0},v\in\mathfrak{h}^{1}.

Remark 5.18
.

Since the stuffle product
\ast
as well as the shuffle product
\shuffle
are graded for the weight, the ideal
\operatorname{R}_{\operatorname{EDS}}
is weight-homogeneous. Therefore, the algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
is weight-graded.

Remark 5.19
.

We won’t compare the formal multiple zeta values to the motivic multiple zeta values, which are formalisations that depend on a different set of relations. Their construction is requires a profound knowledge of modern arithmetic geometry, for detail we refer to
[
10
]
. See also Remark
5.16
.

Let us denote the canonical projection by

\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\to\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}},

(40)

thus
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(w)
is the class of
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
in the quotient algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
. The space
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
is a weight-graded algebra spanned by the elements
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(w)
,
w\in\mathcal{X}^{*}
, which satisfy exactly the extended double shuffle relations. In particular, we have for all
k\geq 1
that

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0}^{k})=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{1}^{k})=0.

(41)

If we have a word
w=x_{0}^{k_{1}-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}-1}x_{1}\in\mathfrak{h}^{0}
, we often write instead of
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(w)
also
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})
.
By Proposition
5.11
and (
41
) these are also a spanning set for
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
.
Additionally, the
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{d})
with
k_{1}\geq 2
also satisfy the usual stuffle product formulas.

By construction, the
evaluation map

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}

\displaystyle\to\Z,

(42)

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(w)

\displaystyle\mapsto\zeta_{\shuffle}(w)

is a surjective algebra morphism.
By Conjecture
5.14
, this map should be an algebra isomorphism.

Proposition 5.20
.

For
k\geq 0
, let
B_{k}
be the
k
-th Bernoulli number and set

b_{n}=(-1)^{n+1}B_{2n}\frac{24^{n}}{2(2n)!}.

Then we have for each
n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n)

\displaystyle=b_{n}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)^{n},

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{6^{n}}{(2n+1)!}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)^{n}.

Here we use the notation
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{n})=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\underbrace{2,\ldots,2}_{n\text{ times}})
.

Proof.

Euler showed by analytic means his famous formula

\zeta(2n)=(-1)^{n+1}\frac{B_{2n}}{2(2n)!}(2\pi)^{2n}=b_{n}\zeta(2)^{n}.

For the formal multiple zeta values we deduce this formula from the extended double shuffle as follows:
Considering the generating series for the formal multiple zeta values in depth two we deduce as Gangl, Kaneko and Zagier in
[
25
]
the identity

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,s\text{ even}\\
r+s=k\end{subarray}}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(r,s)=\frac{3}{4}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k).

Now using
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(r)\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(s)=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(r,s)+\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(s,r)+\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(r+s)

we get for the sum of products

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r,s\text{ even}\\
r+s=k\end{subarray}}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(r)\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(s)=\frac{k+1}{2}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k).

This yields a recursion for the numbers
b_{n}
in the equation
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n)=b_{n}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)^{n}
, whose first terms are

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(4)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{5}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)^{2},

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(6)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{7}\Big(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(4)+\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(4)\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)\Big)=\frac{8}{35}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)^{3}.

Now using a simple manipulation of the generating series for
Bernoulli numbers
3
3

3

It is not difficult to check that
-\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{6}x\operatorname{cot}(\sqrt{6}x)-1\right)
is the generating series for the rational numbers
b_{n}
.
This approach to obtain this formula of Euler in the formal setting is elaborated in
[
5
, Corollary 2.12]
.

or using the evaluation map and Euler’s formula for the even zeta values
we deduce

b_{n}=(-1)^{n+1}B_{2n}\frac{24^{n}}{2(2n)!}.

In their paper
[
27
]
on quasi-shuffle algebras Hofmann and Ihara showed that for all
k\geq 2
we have the identity of generating series

\displaystyle\exp\left(\sum_{i\geq 1}\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{i}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(ik)t^{i}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{k\}^{n})\,t^{n}.

If we specialize this to
k=2
we see that
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{n})
is a polynomial in even formal zeta values, however by the algebraic Euler formula this
equals a rational multiple of
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)^{n}
. As before we can determine this proportionality factor either by a direct calculation or by a comparison with the analytic
identity

\zeta(\{2\}^{n})=\frac{\pi^{2n}}{(2n+1)!}=\frac{6^{n}}{(2n+1)!}\zeta(2)^{n}.

due to Hoffman and Zagier.
∎

Remark 5.21
.

In the above proof we reduced identities for formal multiple zeta values to the determination of a rational number, which we computed by means of the evaluation map. Actually, we could even had calculated it without referring to the previous known analytic identities.
In Subsection
6.2
we see a much more elaborate lift of another set of identities satisfied by multiple zeta values to the formal setting. Those formulae due to Zagier can be seen as a refinement of the following proposition and so far no algebraic proof without referring to analytical identities is known.

Proposition 5.22
.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have the identity

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}((x_{0}x_{1})^{n}x_{0})

\displaystyle=-2\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{i},3,\{2\}^{n-1-i})

(43)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2i+1)\,\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{n-i}).

Proof.

For the first equality we consider the shuffle product
identity in
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle

\displaystyle x_{0}\shuffle(x_{0}x_{1})^{n}=(x_{0}x_{1})^{n}x_{0}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}2\,(x_{0}x_{1})^{i}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}(x_{0}x_{1})^{n-1-i}

and this amounts in the quotient algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
to

\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0})\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{n})=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}((x_{0}x_{1})^{n}x_{0})+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}2\,\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{i},3,\{2\}^{n-1-i}).

The left-hand side vanishes since
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0})=0
and the first identity follows.

For the second equality we use that the multiplication of formal zeta values with admissible indices satisfy the stuffle relation, we thus get

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(3)\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{n-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{i},3,\{2\}^{n-1-i})+\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{i},5,\{2\}^{n-2-i})

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5)\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{n-2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n-2}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{i},5,\{2\}^{n-2-i})+\sum_{i=0}^{n-3}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{i},7,\{2\}^{n-3-i})

\displaystyle\;\vdots

\displaystyle\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n-1)\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n-1,2)+\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2,2n-1)+\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n+1).

By taking the alternating sum we obtain

-\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{i},3,\{2\}^{n-1-i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2i+1)\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\{2\}^{n-i})

and the claim follows.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

The identity (
43
) implies
for the space
\mathcal{B}^{1}
defined in Theorem
4.9
that

\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}\big(\mathcal{B}^{1}\big)
is contained in the vector space
generated by those
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(k_{1},...,k_{d})
, where one
k_{i}
equals
3
and all other
k_{j}
equal
2
.

5.4
Racinet’s approach and Ecalle’s theorem

We explain Racinet’s approach to formal multiple zeta values in terms of non-commutative power series (
[
38
]
). This means, we assign an affine group scheme and a Lie algebra to the formal multiple zeta values. This has two deep, structural consequences for the algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
. For more detailed expositions we refer to
[
13
]
,
[
22
]
.

Definition 5.24
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an alphabet,
R
be some
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra, and
\varphi:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\to R
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear map. Then the non-commutative generating series associated to
\varphi
is

\operatorname{Gen}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{A})=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{A}^{*}}\varphi(w)w\in R\langle\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\rangle.

We assume that
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle,\ast_{\diamond})
is any graded quasi-shuffle algebra with
\deg(a)\geq 1
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Then we can compute the dual coproduct
\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}}
given in (
6
).

Proposition 5.25
.

A
\mathbb{Q}
-linear map
\varphi:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{A}\rangle\to R
is an algebra morphism with respect to
\ast_{\diamond}
if and only if
\operatorname{Gen}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{A})
is grouplike for
\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}}
,

\Delta_{\ast_{\diamond}}(\operatorname{Gen}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{A}))=\operatorname{Gen}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{A})\otimes\operatorname{Gen}_{\varphi}(\mathcal{A}).

∎

Motivated by this proposition, we consider the following sets.

Definition 5.26
.

For any commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
with unit, let
\operatorname{DM}(R)
be the set of all non-commutative power series
\phi\in R\langle\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\rangle
satisfying

(i)

(\phi|x_{0})\ =\ (\phi|x_{1})

=

0
,

(ii)

\Delta_{\shuffle}(\phi)

=

\phi\hat{\otimes}\phi
,

(iii)

\Delta_{\ast}(\phi_{\ast})

=

\phi_{\ast}\hat{\otimes}\phi_{\ast}
,

where

\phi_{\ast}=\exp\left(\sum_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}(\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}(\phi)|y_{n})y_{1}^{n}\right)\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}(\phi)\in R\langle\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle\rangle

and
\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}
is the
R
-linear extension of the projection

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle,

\displaystyle x_{0}^{k_{1}-1}x_{1}\cdots x_{0}^{k_{d}-1}x_{1}x_{0}^{k_{d+1}-1}

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}y_{k_{1}}\cdots y_{k_{d}},&\quad k_{d+1}=1\\
0&\quad\textsf{else}\end{cases}.

For each
\lambda\in R
, denote by
\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R)
the set of all
\phi\in\operatorname{DM}(R)
, which additionally satisfy

\hskip-62.59596pt\text{(iv)}\qquad(\phi|x_{0}x_{1})=\lambda.

By Theorems
5.10
,
5.12
,
5.13
and Proposition
5.25
, the non-commutative generating series of the shuffle regularized multiple zeta values

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gen}_{\zeta_{\shuffle}}(\mathcal{X})=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{X}^{*}}\zeta_{\shuffle}(w)w

is an element in
\operatorname{DM}_{\pi^{2}/6}(\Z)
.

Theorem 5.27
.

(
[
39
, Theorem I]
) For each commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
and
\lambda\in R
, the set
\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R)
is non-empty. ∎

From
[
19
]
and
[
24
]
one deduces that there also exist elements
\phi
in
\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R)
additionally satisfying

\displaystyle(\phi\mid x_{0}^{k}x_{1})=0\quad\text{ for }k\geq 1\text{ even}.

The sets
\operatorname{DM}(R)
and
\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R)
give rise to affine schemes represented by (quotient algebras of)
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
.

Proposition 5.28
.

(
[
38
, p. 107]
)

(i) The functor
\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}\to\mathsf{Sets}
is an affine scheme represented by the algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
of formal multiple zeta values. In particular, for
R\in\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}
there is a bijection

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}}(\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}},R)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{DM}(R),

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{w\in\mathcal{X}^{*}}\varphi(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(w))w.

(ii) The functors
\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}\to\mathsf{Sets}
are an affine schemes represented by the quotient algebras
{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.49501pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.49501pt}{$\displaystyle{(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)-\lambda)}$}}{\raisebox{4.24501pt}{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.24501pt}{$\textstyle{(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)-\lambda)}$}}{\raisebox{3.24501pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.24501pt}{$\scriptstyle{(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)-\lambda)}$}}{\raisebox{2.45833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.45833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)-\lambda)}$}}}
. ∎

\operatorname{DM}_{0}
is often called the
double shuffle group
. To figure out the group structure for the affine scheme
\operatorname{DM}_{0}
, one needs to consider first the corresponding linearized space.

Definition 5.29
.

For any
R\in\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}
, let
\mathfrak{dm}(R)
be the
R
-vector space of all non-commutative polynomials
\psi\in R\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
satisfying

(i)

(\psi\mid x_{0})\ =\ (\psi\mid x_{1})

=

0
,

(ii)

\Delta_{\shuffle}(\psi)

=

\psi\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\psi
,

(iii)

\Delta_{\ast}(\psi_{\ast})

=

\psi_{\ast}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\psi_{\ast}
,

where

\psi_{\ast}=\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}(\psi)+\sum\limits_{n\geq 2}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}(\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}(\psi)\mid y_{n})y_{1}^{n}\in R\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle

and
\Pi_{\mathcal{Y}}
is the
R
-linear extension of the canonical projection
\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\to\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{Y}\rangle
.

By
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}(R)
denote the subspace of all
\psi\in\mathfrak{dm}(R)
additionally satisfying

\hskip-99.58464pt\text{(iv)}\qquad(\psi\mid x_{0}x_{1})=0.

Denote
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}=\mathfrak{dm}_{0}(\mathbb{Q})
. Then one has
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}(R)=\mathfrak{dm}_{0}\otimes R
. The space
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
is often called the
double shuffle Lie algebra
, the name will be justified in Theorem
5.32
.

Example 5.30
.

The space
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
can be computed algorithmically in small weights, see e.g.
[
22
]
,
[
11
]
. One obtains the following elements in
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
up to weight
5

\displaystyle\xi(3)

\displaystyle=[x_{0},[x_{0},x_{1}]]+[[x_{0},x_{1}],x_{1}],

\displaystyle\xi(5)

\displaystyle=[x_{0},[x_{0},[x_{0},[x_{0},x_{1}]]]]+2[[x_{0},[x_{0},[x_{0},x_{1}]]],x_{1}]+\frac{1}{2}[[x_{0},[x_{0},x_{1}]],[x_{0},x_{1}]]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+2[x_{1},[x_{1},[x_{0},[x_{0},x_{1}]]]]-\frac{3}{2}[[x_{0},x_{1}],[[x_{0},x_{1}],x_{1}]]+[[[[x_{0},x_{1}],x_{1}],x_{1}],x_{1}].

Proposition 5.31
.

(
[
38
, IV, Proposition 2.2]
) For each
k\geq 2
even and
\psi\in\mathfrak{dm}_{0}(R)
, one has

(\psi\mid x_{0}^{k-1}x_{1})=0.

∎

The Lie bracket on the spaces
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}(R)
is given by the Ihara bracket
\{-,-\}
from Definition
3.12
.

Theorem 5.32
.

(
[
38
, IV, Proposition 2.28., Corollary 3.13.]
) Let
R
be a commutative
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra with unit.

1) The space
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}(R)
is a Lie subalgebra of
(\mathfrak{f}_{2}\otimes R,\{-,-\})
.

2) For all
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R)
, there exists a unique element
\psi
in the completed Lie algebra
\widehat{\mathfrak{dm}_{0}}(R)
such that

\exp(s_{\psi})(\phi_{1})=\phi_{2}.

∎

Here for any
f\in R\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
,
s_{f}:R\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\to R\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
is the
R
-linear map defined by
s_{f}(w)=d_{f}(w)+fw
. Note that this allows to write

\displaystyle\{f,g\}=s_{f}(g)-s_{g}(f),\qquad f,g\in R\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle.

A more detailed proof of Theorem
5.32
1) is given in
[
24
, Appendix A]
and also in the first author’s master thesis
[
11
]
.

From Theorem
5.32
2), one obtains natural bijections

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathfrak{dm}_{0}}(R)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{DM}_{0}(R),

(44)

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(s_{\psi})(\mathbf{1}).

We deduce the following.

Corollary 5.33
.

(
[
38
]
) The functor
\operatorname{DM}_{0}
is a pro-unipotent affine group scheme with Lie algebra functor

\widehat{\mathfrak{dm}_{0}}:\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-Alg}\to\operatorname{Lie-Alg},\ R\mapsto\widehat{\mathfrak{dm}_{0}}(R).

∎

The group multiplication on
\operatorname{DM}_{0}(R)
is given in Theorem
3.18
or Remark
3.21
, so
\operatorname{DM}_{0}(R)
is a subgroup of
(\operatorname{Grp}(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}\otimes R)),\circledast)
.

Moreover, it is shown by Racinet that the affine schemes
\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}
are
\operatorname{DM}_{0}
-torsors.

Theorem 5.34
.

(
[
38
, Section IV, Corollary 3.13]
)
The affine group scheme
\operatorname{DM}_{0}
acts freely and transitively on the affine schemes
\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}
by left multiplication. ∎

In other words, for each
R\in\mathbb{Q}\operatorname{-}\mathsf{Alg}
we obtain a map

\displaystyle\circledast:\operatorname{DM}_{0}(R)\times\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R)

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R),

\displaystyle(\Phi,G)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Phi\circledast G,

such that
-\circledast G:\operatorname{DM}_{0}(R)\to\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R)
is bijective for any
G\in\operatorname{DM}_{\lambda}(R)
. Combining those maps gives rise to natural maps

\displaystyle\circledast:\operatorname{DM}_{0}(R)\times\operatorname{DM}(R)\to\operatorname{DM}(R).

(45)

An application of Yoneda’s Lemma to the isomorphism
\exp:\widehat{\mathfrak{dm}_{0}}\to\operatorname{DM}_{0}
of affine schemes given in (
44
), yields the following main result.

Corollary 5.35
.

(
[
38
]
, Chapter IV, Corollary 3.14) There is an algebra isomorphism

\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)]\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{dm}_{0})^{\vee}.

By (
7
), we have proved Ecalle’s theorem
[
21
]
.

Theorem 5.36
.

The algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
of formal multiple zeta values is a free polynomial algebra.

5.5
Goncharov coproduct and Goncharov-Brown coaction

Let

\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}=\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}/(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2))

be the quotient algebra of
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
by the principal ideal generated by
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)
and write

\mathsf{a}:\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}

for the natural projection. We define a coproduct on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}
by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(a)=\Big(\mathsf{a}\circ\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}\otimes\,\mathsf{a}\circ\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}\Big)\big(\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(w)\big),

(46)

where
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
is a lift for
a\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}
, i.e.
w
is any element such that
\mathsf{a}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(w))=a
.

Theorem 5.37
.

The triple
\big(\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}},\cdot,\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}\big)
is a weight-graded Hopf algebra.

Proof.

In Corollary
5.33
, we have seen that
\operatorname{DM}_{0}
is a subgroup scheme of the affine group scheme given in (
25
) induced by the grouplike elements of
(\widehat{\mathcal{U}}(\mathfrak{f}_{2}),\Delta_{\shuffle})
. Since
\operatorname{DM}_{0}
is represented by the algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}
(Proposition
5.28
), and the affine group scheme in (
25
) is represented by the Hopf algebra
(\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}})
(Lemma
3.25
, Proposition
3.26
), also
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}
must be a Hopf algebra. The coproduct on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}
is induced by the Goncharov coproduct under the projection

\mathsf{a}\circ\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}:\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle\to\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}.\qed

Remark 5.38
.

In particular we deduce from the proof of the above theorem that the coproduct (
46
) is well-defined.
We like to point to the fact that our approach
to the coproduct relies on the general theory of post-Lie algebras together with the work of Racinet, whereas
the original definition of Goncharov in
[
26
]

was based on topological considerations for the path algebra. The latter is directly related to the representation of multiple zeta values by iterated integrals.

Following Proposition
2.29
, the Goncharov coproduct
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}
induces a Lie cobracket
\delta
on the space of indecomposables
\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{f}}
of the quotient algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}
.
By Proposition
2.37
, there is a canonical isomorphism of Lie algebras

\mathfrak{dm}_{0}\simeq(\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{f}})^{\vee}.

Summarizing the previous results leads to the following diagram, which should be seen as a special case of the diagram (
9
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}},\cdot,\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{dm}_{0}),\circledast,\Delta_{\shuffle}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\operatorname{DM}_{0},\circledast\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{f}},\delta\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\mathfrak{dm}_{0},\{-,-\}\big)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
graded dual
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1:1}
Prop
5.28
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
graded dual
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1:1}
Cor
5.33

(47)

Definition 5.39
.

Mimicking the construction of Brown
[
9
]
we define a coaction

\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}:\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}\otimes\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}

on the whole algebra
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
of formal multiple zeta values by setting

\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\xi)=\Big(\mathsf{a}\circ\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}\otimes\,\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}\Big)\big(\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(w)\big),

where
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
is a lift for
\xi
, i.e.
w
is any element such that
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(w)=\xi
.

Theorem 5.40
.

The coaction
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}:\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}\to\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}\otimes\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
is well-defined.

Proof.

By (
45
), we have a morphism of affine schemes

\displaystyle\operatorname{DM}_{0}\times\operatorname{DM}

\displaystyle\to\operatorname{DM}

\displaystyle(\Phi,G)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Phi\circledast G.

Applying Yoneda’s Lemma (Theorem
2.32
) yields an algebra morphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}\otimes\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}},

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big(\mathsf{a}\circ\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}\otimes\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}\Big)\big(\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(w)\big),

where
w\in\mathbb{Q}\langle\mathcal{X}\rangle
satisfies
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(w)=\xi
. The group structure of
\operatorname{DM}_{0}
gives rise to the coassociativity and the counitarity of this morphism.
∎

By Example
3.28
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{1},

and hence we obtain

\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2))=1\otimes\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}\otimes\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}.

(48)

This coaction is an extension of
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}
on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}
given in (
46
) to
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
.
In order to give an explicit formula for it we set for
\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{n+1}\in\mathcal{X}
and a word
\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{*}

\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\varepsilon_{0};\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n};\varepsilon_{n+1})=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(I(\varepsilon_{0};\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n};\varepsilon_{n+1})),

and then by Definition
3.24
for the Goncharov coproduct we get the explicit formula

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\varepsilon_{1}\cdots\varepsilon_{n}))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq n\\
0<i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}<n+1\end{subarray}}\ \prod_{p=0}^{k}\mathsf{a}\big(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\varepsilon_{i_{p}};\varepsilon_{i_{p}+1}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}-1};\varepsilon_{i_{p+1}})\big)\otimes\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}})

(49)

where
i_{0}=0,\,i_{k+1}=n+1
and
\varepsilon_{0}=x_{1},\,\varepsilon_{n+1}=x_{0}
.

Remark 5.41
.

In Definition
5.39
, it is necessary to consider
\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{f}}
instead of
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
in the first tensor product factor.
Recall from Example
3.28
that

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{1}

and therefore on the one hand side we get

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{1}\shuffle x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{1})^{\shuffle 2}=(x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{1})^{\shuffle 2}

\displaystyle=(x_{0}x_{1}\shuffle x_{0}x_{1})\otimes\mathbf{1}+2\,x_{0}x_{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes(x_{0}x_{1}\shuffle x_{0}x_{1}).

On the other side we have

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1})

\displaystyle=x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}-2x_{0}x_{0}\otimes x_{0}x_{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes x_{0}x_{0}x_{0}x_{1}.

Since
\frac{2}{5}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)^{2}=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(4)
as shown in Proposition
5.20
, both expressions should coincide up to the scalar
\frac{2}{5}
after passing to formal multiple zeta values. However, the terms in the middle differ as
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0}x_{1})=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)\neq 0
but
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0}x_{0})=0
.

Example 5.42
.

We want to compute
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5))
by using the graphical interpretation of ”eating worms” (cf. Figure
2
). Since
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5)=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0}^{4}x_{1})
we consider subwords of
x_{0}^{4}x_{1}
. The terms in (
49
) corresponding to
\mathbf{1}
and
x_{0}^{4}x_{1}
are visualized, respectively, as

and we obtain the summands
\mathsf{a}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5))\otimes\mathbf{1}
and
\mathbf{1}\otimes\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5)
, respectively. Now consider the terms that are depicted with a ”red worm” ending on the right bound
x_{0}
, e. g.

The factor that arises from this ”red worm” vanishes as the left bound is also
x_{0}
and thus the summand does not contribute to
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5))
. Similarly, the factors that correspond to a ”red worm” starting in any
x_{0}
vanish, e. g.

Note that if the worm ends in
x_{1}
the factor is
(-1)^{i}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0}^{i})
for some
i\leq 3
which vanishes by (
41
). Hence only the first two terms contribute non-trivially and we obtain

\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5))=\mathsf{a}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5))\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(5)

This argument can be generalized to show the following lemma.

Lemma 5.43
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n+1))=\mathsf{a}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n+1))\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n+1).

Proof.

Since
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n+1)=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0}^{2n}x_{1})
each summand in
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n+1)
corresponds to a subword of
x_{0}^{2n}x_{1}
(cf. Definition
5.39
).
Similar to Example
5.42
, the subwords
\mathbf{1}
and
x_{0}^{2n}x_{1}
contribute with
\mathsf{a}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n+1))\otimes\mathbf{1}
and
\mathbf{1}\otimes\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2n+1)
, respectively. It thus suffices to show that all other subwords of
x_{0}^{2n}x_{1}
contribute trivially.
So let
k\in\{1,\dots,2n\}
and let
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}
be a subword of
x_{0}^{2n}x_{1}
.
If
i_{k}<2n+1
, then
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=x_{0}^{k}
and the term vanishes since
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0}^{k})=0
by (
41
).
Thus, we restrict to the case
i_{k}=2n+1
, i. e.
\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=x_{1}
. If
k=1
then the summand vanishes since
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{1})=0
.
If
k>1
then
\varepsilon_{i_{1}}\cdots\varepsilon_{i_{k}}=x_{0}^{k-1}x_{1}
and the product in the formula for
\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}
contains the factor
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(x_{0}^{l})=0
for some
l>0
. Hence all other subwords contribute trivially.
∎

5.6
On the free odd generation conjecture

Central for this notes is the following well-known conjecture for
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
, which is motivated by conjectures of Deligne (
[
17
]
) and Y. Ihara (
[
32
, p. 300]
)
in the context of certain Galois actions and of Drinfeld
[
19
]
on his Grothendieck-Teichmüller Lie algebra. By work of Furusho
[
24
]
, we know that the Grothendieck-Teichmüller Lie algebra embedds into
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
.

Conjecture 5.44
.

The double shuffle Lie algebra
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
is a free Lie algebra with exactly one generator in each odd weight
w\geq 3
, i.e.

\mathfrak{dm}_{0}\simeq\Lie(S)

where
S=\{s_{3},s_{5},\ldots,s_{2n+1},\ldots\}
.

We call this conjecture the
free odd generation conjecture.

Remark 5.45
.

Of course the Lie algebra

\Lie(S)

is also in the heart of the theory of motivic multiple zeta values (
[
9
]
,
[
17
]
,
[
26
]
,
[
16
]
).
It occurs as the Lie algebra of the motivic fundamental group of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
and in fact Brown proved that it also equals the Lie algebra of the motivic Galois group attached to the category of mixed Tate motives.

An immediate consequence of the free odd generation conjecture  would be the truth of Zagier’s dimension conjecture
5.2
for formal multiple zeta values.

Proposition 5.46
.

Under the assumption of the free odd generation conjecture,  one obtains

H_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}(x)=\sum_{w\geq 0}\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}_{w}x^{w}=\frac{1}{1-x^{2}-x^{3}}.

Proof.

Under the assumption of the free odd generation conjecture
5.44
, one obtains the following Hilbert-Poincare series for the universal enveloping algebra of
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}

\displaystyle H_{\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{dm}_{0})}(x)=\frac{1}{1-x^{3}-x^{5}-x^{7}-\dots}.

By Corollary
5.35
, there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)]\otimes\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{dm}_{0})^{\vee}
. Hence, we deduce

\displaystyle H_{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}(x)=H_{\mathbb{Q}[\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)]}(x)H_{\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{dm}_{0})}(x)=\frac{1}{1-x^{2}}\frac{1}{1-x^{3}-x^{5}-x^{7}-\cdots}=\frac{1}{1-x^{2}-x^{3}}.

∎

Recall from Example
2.43
, that we have for the Lie algebra in the free odd generation conjecture
5.44

\mathcal{U}(\Lie(S))=(\mathbb{Q}\langle s_{3},s_{5},\ldots\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\shuffle}),

and the graded dual is given by

\mathcal{U}^{\vee}=(\mathbb{Q}\langle s_{3},s_{5},s_{7},\dots\rangle,\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}).

In Definition
2.44
we defined
\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}=\mathcal{U}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[s_{2}]

and extended the deconcatenation coproduct on
\mathcal{U}^{\vee}
to a coaction

\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}:\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}\to\mathcal{U}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}

via
\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}(s_{2})=\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{2}
.
We now also set
s_{2n}=b_{n}s_{2}^{n}
, where the rational numbers
b_{n}
are given in Proposition
5.20
.

Theorem 5.47
.

Assume the free odd generation conjecture
5.44
for
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
. There is an isomorphism of algebras with coaction

\Phi\colon(\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}},\cdot,\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}},\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})

(50)

satisfying for each
N\geq 3
odd

\Phi(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(N))=s_{N}.

Proof.

Assume the free odd generation conjecture
5.44
for
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
, then
from Theorem
3.8
applied to the Ihara bracket, we get a Hopf algebra isomorphism

(\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{dm}_{0}),\circledast,\Delta_{\shuffle}))\cong\mathcal{U}(\Lie(S))=(\mathbb{Q}\langle s_{3},s_{5},\ldots\rangle,\operatorname{conc},\Delta_{\shuffle}).

By Corollary
5.35
and by dualization we have
a Hopf algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\Big({\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.49501pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.49501pt}{$\displaystyle{(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2))}$}}{\raisebox{4.24501pt}{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.24501pt}{$\textstyle{(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2))}$}}{\raisebox{3.24501pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.24501pt}{$\scriptstyle{(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2))}$}}{\raisebox{2.45833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-2.45833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2))}$}}},\ \cdot\ ,\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}\Big)\simeq(\mathcal{U}^{\vee},\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}}).

(51)

It is a direct consequence of Corollary
5.35
and the compatibily of the construction of the coaction on
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}
via (
48
) and that on
\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}
by Definition
2.44
that we can extend (
51
) to an isomorphism of algebras with coaction
\Phi:(\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}},\cdot,\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}},\shuffle,\Delta_{\operatorname{dec}})
.

Since we have for any odd
N\geq 3

\displaystyle\Delta_{\operatorname{Gon}}(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(N))

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(N)\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(N),

\displaystyle\Delta_{\text{dec}}(s_{N})

\displaystyle=s_{N}\otimes\mathbf{1}+\mathbf{1}\otimes s_{N},

the isomorphism
\Phi
can be chosen such that
\Phi(\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(N))=s_{N}
.
∎

Corollary 5.48
.

Assume the free odd generation conjecture
5.44
for
\mathfrak{dm}_{0}
. The formal zeta values
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)
and
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2r+1)
,
r\in\mathbb{N}
, are non-zero modulo products and algebraically independent.

Proof.

Evidently, the letters
s_{2r+1}
are nonzero modulo products and algebraically independent in
\mathcal{U}^{\vee}
. So the same must hold for their preimages
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2r+1)=\Phi^{-1}(s_{2r+1})
under the isomorphism (
50
). By construction of
\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}=\mathcal{U}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[s_{2}]
and the convention
\zeta^{\mathsf{f}}(2)=\Phi^{-1}(s_{2})
, the claim follows.
∎

Remark 5.49
.

By work of Drinfeld
[
19
]
, Brown
[
9
]
, and Furusho
[
24
]
, we have inclusions

\Lie(S)\subset\mathfrak{grt}_{1}\subset\mathfrak{dm}_{0}.

This implies
\mathcal{U}(\Lie(S))\subset\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{dm}_{0})
, and hence
\Phi:\mathcal{Z}^{\mathsf{f}}\to\mathcal{U}^{\mathsf{f}}
is surjective. In particular, we obtain Corollary
5.48
without assuming the free odd generation conjecture.

5.7
The Kernel conjecture

Recall that in Subsection
2.4
we introduced the derivations
D_{w}
and their extension to algebras with particular coaction.
With the notation from Subsection
3.6
and
5.5
we get the commutative diagram

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
